When the first Wycaro book was being published the art department hated Carol's guts because she wouldn’t stop sending correction after correction after correction about what Raban should look like on the cover.

    The eye color needs to be warmer, the jaw needs to be sharper, the hair needs to fall in more careless waves, like Raban just woke up and doesn’t care that there are other people around who might be affected by how stunning she looks so early in the morning and need a minute before they can speak. 

     

     

    Once they got it right, once the person on the cover was basically just Zosia with thicker eye brows, a mustache and goatee, Carol lost interest and just said, “Yeah that's good.”

    The whole art department had been working tirelessly on this thing, day and night to make Carol happy and the moment they got it right she just…lost interest. It was almost like Carol couldn’t even bear to look at the book, the art department had been very confused by the whole thing to say the least. 

    Her and Helen never fought about Zosia, there was no need. Carol didn’t love her anymore, she never really got the chance too anyway, what she had with Helen was different and lived in. They built a life together that Carol never thought she’d be able to have, that she never thought she would deserve. Carol would never trade it for anything, she would never love anyone the way she loved Helen. With her whole heart and without fear.

    But sometimes Zosia was there, there in the room when Carol wrote her books and Carol could feel the phantom of her over her shoulder as she wrote, whispering into Carol’s ear with her lips brushing against Carol's skin like she always let them. “I would never do that,” The ghost would say, “You're shivering. I would give you my coat and use it to pull you into my arms and kiss you until you stopped speaking.” 

    Sometimes in the long lines for book signings when the faces blurred together and Carol's purple sharpie moved without thought, she would let herself wonder what it would be like if Zosia was in the line. If Zosia would throw a copy of the book down in front of Carol and confront her about all the bullshit she had written. 

    Sometimes Carol would wonder if Zosia even knew about the book at all. If she knew that Carol’s dreams had come true and she had gotten herself not only published but on the best sellers list. Maybe Zosia had read the book. Maybe she had seen it on a shelf somewhere and her eyes had run by it only to jerk back at the sight of Carol's name and her own face staring back at her. Maybe Zosia had picked it up and read it as quickly as she could, desperate to feel connected to Carol, to know what had become of her. 

    Maybe Zosia read the book and though it was crap and showed it to her friends and laughed about how pathetic Carol was and said that a long time ago she fucked the author and now she was writing fantasy novels about Zosia tens years later. Tchórz.

    Maybe Zosia read the book and loved it, maybe she read the book and understood it for what it was. An apology. A wish. A possible life left unfulfilled.

    But Zosia was never in the lines at her readings. 





    2018, 7 years,  2 weeks, and 17 days before the Joining.

    Carol gets her second DUI when she’s driving home a friend from the golf club. Helen was always nervous about Carol meeting up with this group. Carol always came home from these events drunker than usual. But this time, one of the women had agreed to be their designated driver and to pick everyone up so no one would get behind the wheel. Everyone was a little more cautious after Carol’s accident.

    When they had gotten to the club the whole group did a toast in celebration and pushed the driver to have one as well, “You can’t ‘cheers’ with water!” They all called out with ‘boo’s.’ The driver had caved and one drink turned into two turned into 5. By the end of the night somehow the most sober person was Carol, maybe just because she had more tolerance but also because she actually hadn’t drank that much. She too had been trying to be more cautious since her accident and Helen’s watchful eyes had started lingering a little too long. 

    Carol drove four of the women home with no problems. She was on the way to the last and had decided to never hang out with them again because none of them could hold their booze and she had grown sick of them all, more and more, as the night went on. They were in the final stretch of highway before the turn off when Carol saw the flashing lights in the rearview. She’d sat up straighter and taken off her flannel leaving her in just a white t-shirt, she found a random lipstick in the glove compartment and applied it as quickly and neatly as she could and fluffed up her hair. 

    Carol had thought it would be fine, she truly wasn’t feeling drunk at all, the woman next to her was so obviously out of it she thought the contrast would work in her favor. But the cop insisted he smelled alcohol on her breath and made her take a breathalyzer. 0.085

    Carol calls Helen to come bail her out, her voice hushed on the phone, ashamed. When Helen gets her they barely speak, she brings Carol a coat and some hand sanitizer but doesn’t say a word. Carol starts talking when they get into the car saying that it wasn’t her fault, that Pam was supposed to stay sober and refused to leave her car, that she dropped everyone else off without an issue, that she wasn’t even pulled over because she was driving erratically she was just 5 mph over the speed limit and the cop was being a dick. That she was barely drinking, she was just barely over the limit. Helen doesn’t respond to any of this, occasionally she scoffs or rolls her eyes but mostly she just looks straight out the windshield, hands firmly at ten and two. 

    After a few minutes of silence Carol hesitantly looks over at Helen, scared of making things worse and asks, “Is the arrest going to leak anywhere?”

    “No,” Helen says firmly, her voice heavy with concealed anger, but still trying to be comforting.

    “Why?” Carol asks confused, it's her second DUI in 10 years, she’s sure there's a market for that gossip even if she is just a romance novelist, she’s on the best sellers list for gods sake. 

    Helen finally cracks a smile, she lifts a hand off the wheel and props her elbow up on the door next to her, leaning her head into the hand as she lets out a gleeful laugh. “What’s so funny?” Carol asks a little offended, a little revealed the anger is lightening.

    “Officer Thomas’ wife is a huge Wycaro fan. She loves Raban.” Helen looks over to her knowing how much Carol will hate that and smiling at the disgust on Carol's face. “I promised that you’d take her out to lunch, give her a copy of the first chapter of the next book, and answer any question she has ever had about the books.”

    “You’re joking,” Carol says, her face blank. 

    “Nope,” Helen says, the ‘P’ popping as she makes a turn. Carol watches her for a moment, pissed but mostly impressed. 

    “Wow.”

    “Uh huh,” Helen says happily.

    “It’s almost worse than the DUI,” Carol tries to joke.

    Helen’s smile falls, her eyes dart over to Carol in warning, “Don’t push it.”





    2019, 6 years, 10 months, 1 week, and 6 days before the Joining. 

    Carol has been stalling on her fourth Winds of Wycaro book. She doesn’t want to do another book tour, they’ve been wearing her out more and more as time goes on. She’s tired of pretending for people, she’s tired of writing her main character moon over a man, as time has gone by Raban has become more and more unrecognizable to her.

    She’s tired of writing books she doesn’t feel proud of or that she can’t let herself feel proud of. She’s tired of spending four months traveling across the country and now a month internationally to sell a book about a poor man's version of her ex-girlfriend in front of her wife. 

    One night, desperate for something new, she tried to dig up her novel from Berlin. She was able to get through the first ten pages before she had to put it away, still unable to look at it. But maybe she could rework the idea into something else?

    She’s writing now, or trying to write but nothing is working. Raban has just betrayed the crew in order to protect Lucasia and Carol can’t figure out what to do next. What punishment would be enough to be taken seriously but not so much to separate him from Lucasia for more than 50 pages? She tries to write without thinking about it, to see where the story takes her, before she knows it Raban is walking the plank and Lucasia is staring down at the ripple of sand hiding her tears. Can she kill Raban? She stalls her fingers over the keys. Would they let her kill him? He’d have to come back eventually right? Or the readers would completely abandon her. But if he was gone for a while… she could write about Lucasia more. She could write about more than just a love story again. She could write about a woman who rebuilds herself stronger after a loss and finds a new purpose. 

    She decides to polish up the draft of the chapter over the course of the next day, just to see if she could get it to a place that works. When she finishes she anxiously waits by her printer as every page slowly comes out. She staples it together like something fragile and walks down the hall with it to find Helen. 

    Carol passes it over without a word and continues to the kitchen to distract herself, Helen is used to this sort of behavior by now. Used to how antsy Carol gets when she has a new chapter she isn’t sure about. Used to the dance of Carol hiding her excitement but needing praise. 

    Carol is fumbling around with their juicer when Helen finally comes into the kitchen and sets the chapter down on the counter beside her. She takes a seat at the bar next to Carol. “Well?” Carol asks desperately, waiting for the silence to be filled.

    “It’s great, very well written, every beat of it works.” Carol smiles, relieved. “Will he come back later?” Helen asks hesitantly as she flips through the pages.

    “Of course,” Carol says as she stands and rubs her hands over her pants nervously. “I just thought,” she gestures, searching for the word. “They’ve never been apart, Raban and Lucasia, it would be interesting to see what happens, who Lucasia is when she isn’t swooning over Raban. Who she could become without him,” Carol’s excitement is palpable. “But obviously he will come back, I’m not sure how yet but I know he can't die die.”

    “Of course,” Helen says with a smile. She presses a kiss into the side of Carol’s head. “I’m sure absence will only make the heart grow fonder after all.” Helen jokes as she walks away, her phone has started ringing in the other room. She turns back before she leaves the room, her eyes firm and uplifting, “It’s your story babe. You take it wherever you think it needs to go. We’ll figure out the rest after.” Her voice is warm and uplifting, supportive.

    Carol smiles and picks up the chapter, finally excited to work on it again and heads back into her office, relieved by Helen’s encouragement. The perks of marrying your manager not your editor.





    2025, 4 months and 2 days before the Joining.

    Carol is running around the house looking for her phone charger and her glasses case. She had only finished packing last night and had forgotten to grab them, mostly because she had no clue where they were. Helen is waiting by the door with her bags neatly packed, one hand on the rolly bags handle, the other on her hip as she smiles with all the love in the world in her eyes watching Carol run from room to room in their house looking for various items she had forgotten to grab.

    “My laptop!” Carol calls out.

    “Packed!” Helen calls back.

    “The stupid purple sharpies?”

    “Packed!”

    Carol slowly peaks around a corner, sheepish, she walks slowly towards Helen. “My noise cancelling headphones. Helen pats her bag, “Packed.” Her eyes running up and down Carol’s body as she approaches. 

    “What about my reading glasses?” 

    Helen smiles as Carol stops just a step away. Her hand reaches out to Carol's breast pocket, her fingers closing around said glasses with a raised eyebrow.

    “Asshole,” Carol says as she pulls Helen into her and kisses her. It’s steady and familiar, they’ve been doing this for almost twenty years now, every time they travel. The routine of it is comforting and grounding and intimate. 

    Carol holds Helen’s face in her hands, Helen’s hands hold Carol’s hips pulling her close and pressing one last kiss to her lips. “Thank you for coming with me.” Carol says gently.

    Helen scoffs, “Like I’d miss this? I can’t wait to see Maureen's face when her beloved Raban walks the plank.” Carol laughs to cover the fear she feels about the fans turning on her. Helen brushes her hair behind her ear and lets her palm settle against Carol's cheek. “They’re going to love it.” Helen pauses letting the words sink in. “‘The best Wycaro yet’ I think the Guardian called it.”

    Carol holds Helen’s head firmer between her hands, twenty years of gratitude pouring out of her into Helen. Carol knows she is difficult, she knows she complains, she knows she is angry and abrasive. But Helen has never made her feel any of that. When it's the two of them Carol never feels heavy, she never feels burdensome, she feels loved. She’s excited for the book tour because she gets to spend every day by Helen's side. Every day with eyes seeking out to Helen’s and finding nothing but unconditional support and affection. Twenty years and Carol is itching for more, twenty years and she can wait for the next. Twenty years and it still feels like just the beginning.

     

     

     

     

    2025, 5 minutes after the Joining

    Carol is leaning over Helen’s body, “Hi baby,” she whimpers out, her voice, devastated.

    She feels the moment Helen goes. Her face is slack, Helen’s hand clutched in hers loses its grip. Helen’s eyes fade of all life and warmth and love. For the first time ever, they stare back at Carol, empty. Carol watches on in terror. What is happening? She begs. She must have it wrong. She lowers her head to Helen’s chest to listen for her heart beat. “No, no, no.” Carol says, pleading with Helen. She brings a hand up to her mouth wiping it over her wet face. She can't die, she can’t. Carol won’t let her.

    She runs her hands over Helen's ribs counting down over the base of Helen’s sternum. She links her fingers over Helen’s chest, a squeaking sob escaping her as she does. How did they get here? She presses down, the tears falling at the motion as sobs gasp out of her. She pumps out maybe ten beats before she takes her hands off Helen’s chest and brings her fingers to her wife's neck. Her wife. She doesn't feel a pulse. She leans down over Helen's mouth to listen for breath. Nothing. She presses her head to Helens’ chest, praying that she is wrong. Nothing. 

    “Goddamn it.” Carol cries.

    She forces herself to keep it together and presses her hands back over Helen’s chest and resumes CPR, she doesn't even know if she’s doing it right. It's been years since she had any training on this, should she be blowing air into Helen's mouth? Was she supposed to tilt her head back if she did? Why did she not get re-certified in this? Why did she drag Helen out to the bar? They could have been at home, laying on the couch like she knew Helen wanted to be when whatever this is, started. The sight of Helen’s prone body falling replays in her mind as she chokes on the tears filling her mouth and keeps pressing her hands into Helen’s still heart. 

    She stops when she feels one of Helen’s ribs give under the pressure of her compressions. She reels back from her and tears her hands off Helen’s body, disgusted with herself. Her eyes trace over Helen’s face, her slack face, her empty face. “Somebody please help me,” Carol whispers out, barely audible over the wind. “No, no, no. No, no don’t.” She cries as she leans back over Helen's body and begs her to stay. “Please. Please.” She drops her head down in shame, she failed her. In her final moments she had failed Helen, just like she always knew she would. 

    Helen had spent years protecting Carol from herself, but who was protecting Helen from Carol?

    She lifts her head and looks at the face of the woman she loves more than life itself. She pushes the hair delicately out of Helen’s face, trying to be as gentle with her as Helen was with Carol, trying not to think of her crushed ribs. She sobs at the feeling of Helen’s face against her hands, her still warm skin. She leans down and presses her lips to Helen’s temple, the tears dripping off her cheeks and onto Helen's. She raises her head back up and gathers the lapels of the jacket she bought for Helen all those years ago, and she covers her wife. She lets a hand rest on Helen's neck, a final moment of hope for a pulse rushes through her and is killed just as quickly. What if she missed something? Maybe there’s still a chance. Maybe she’s wrong. 

    Her eyes are forced away from Helen by movement all around her. Everyone else has stopped seizing too. They’re moving out of the hospital. In sync? They turn in perfect arches, moving one after the other in neat lines out the doors. She looks back down at Helen, her heart jumping at the thought that Helen will get up with the others, but there are no small mercies, not for Carol. Helen lays still beside her. Her eyes staring blankly up into the sky. Carol feels another sob build in her throat but she shoves it down as the crowd from the hospital walks by the car. Even now, she can’t let herself just be.

    She crawls out of the truck bed, watching as Dr. Nguyen moves with the crowd. Maybe with a doctor they’d have a chance. They could get a defibrillator, they could get… something. She follows him, weaving through the mass of people. “Dr. Nguyen?” Carol calls out, her voice strained. The whole street turns with him. They all stare at her with matching looks of confusion. She steps back in fear. The doctor steps closer. She forces herself to stand her ground, needing his help. He grabs her by the shoulders and forces his lips against hers. She shoves him off and wipes away his saliva, spitting it onto the ground beside her. 

    “What is happening?” She screams at the top of her lungs at them. 





    The rest of the night is one nightmare after another. The freaks trying to grab Helen, the chaos of the city's streets, the kids outside her door, the body collectors. The man on the TV. “Your life is your own.” The casual way he explained it was only a matter of time before they forced her to join them. Before they found a way to infect her with whatever, ‘not quite a virus’, had spread across the globe. 

    Helen’s voice on the answering machine.

    Is that the last time Carol will ever hear it? Is that the last time she’ll ever hear it? Carol cries on the couch clutching her golf club until she physically can’t anymore. She walks through the dark house to get water. Her eyes catching on Helen's body in the entryway, just as she left her. She stands there for a moment just watching. Her first thought is that Helen looks cold, her second is remembering that Helen will never be cold again. 

    The anger and the guilt and the fear eat at her as she forces herself to stare at her wife. She turns back to the living room and does what she does best. She drinks. 



     

     

     

    It will be two years before Carol gets sober for good. It will be three years until the nightmare ends. It will be 16 hours until Zosia walks into her backyard. It will be a lifetime of wishing that she hadn’t run after that truck.

     

     

     

     

    Chapter End Notes

    For those who read chapter 12 I have deleted it because it didn't fit with my new outline, sorry for the constant changes this week, I've been trying to work out in real time where to take this story and things have been a little chaotic to say the least. I'm feeling very happy with my new plan and am excited to get the next chapter out, still planning for the 16th!

    Thanks for bearing with me :)

    Chapter 12: Part Two: The Return

    Chapter Notes

    hello all, in honor of Rhea's golden globe win and the previously deleted chapter 12 I'm posting early and often (two new chapters) hope you all enjoy :)

    2029, 4 years, 7 months, 2 weeks, and 17 days since the Unjoining.

     

    The street is loud around her, people are streaming by, completely unaware of Carol’s presence; completely unconcerned with her existence. As she walks a man bumps into her shoulder and calls back over his, “Sorry!” without even stopping.

    Before, Carol would have yelled something back at him or spent at least an hour ranting about it to Helen. Now, she just smiles, ecstatic to be ignored, to not be cared about, to not be coddled.

    Carol keeps moving, she tries to walk everywhere now. She likes being on the street surrounded by people, she likes feeling like part of the crowd, she likes the way the world moves in spontaneous bursts and starts. She likes weaving through people without any idea where they are going. She loves the moment when two people try to walk by each other but they both step to the right and then they both step to the left. The idiosyncrasies of life! Every moment that the people around her don’t know her name or her favorite food or how old she was when she stopped wetting the bed, still feels like a novelty. 

    She weaves through people on the sidewalk, passing strollers and dog walkers, passing restaurants full of people and bars where a crowd is screaming about whatever game is on the screens. She passes a couple arguing on the street about the man’s mother who has overstayed her welcome. She passes teenagers who run out of a shop, blocking the whole sidewalk as they giggle and scream over each other. Everywhere Carol looks, there are people living their lives. Living their individual lives.

    Carol is on her way to a bookstore, she has a reading tonight. Her first reading since the world came back, since everyone went from ‘We’ to ‘I’ again. Her first reading without Helen by her side, her first reading without Helen there to get everyone to clap, her first reading without Helen there to smile at her when she gets nervous. She had never done a reading without Helen in the room and she felt unsettled by the idea. 

    Carol can feel the anxiety coursing through her body, the way her stomach feels tight, the way her fingers have started picking at her cuticles. But her feet carry her closer and closer to the bookstore. She didn’t even bring a copy of the book with her, she noticed halfway into her walk that she had forgotten it on the kitchen counter. She didn’t even bring a bag with her, Helen always carried her books, Helen always brought markers and little cards to write peoples names on so that the signing would go faster. 

    Carol had spent the whole day trying to convince herself that this reading and this book were no different than the others, that it was the same as Wycaro. But everything about it felt different, more intimate, more revealing.

    For starters the book was explicitly gay, no room for subtext or hiding. It was a love story between two women, technically three women. Carol had waited patiently for the outrage from her fans when she published the book but none over came. If it did, her publisher did a good job of hiding it from her. Instead what she received was letter after letter from queer people all over the world telling her how much the book meant to them. How the book helped them come to terms with their sexuality or come to terms with how the Joining had impacted their lives. 

    Carol tries everyday not to think about her reading the book. But with every passing moment it gets harder to imagine that she hasn’t or that she hasn’t at least heard about the book. It feels worse than when Carol was writing Wycaro and using Raban to process her relationship with… her.

    When Carol had started writing again after the Joining was over, she tried to write a book that excluded her but no matter what, the story always led the main character to the same place. It felt like Carol couldn’t get any words to work unless they were about her.

    Carol felt terrified of what awaited her at the reading, her first real interaction with her new audience since the book's release. Her first face to face encounter with the world she had spent her whole life hiding from. Carol felt sure that she would be there, waiting.

    Waiting to tell Carol what a monster she was, how she had stolen her face for yet another book character, how she had taken her agency away twice before already and now Carol had done it again in some idiotic novel that she let the world market as a love story.

    Carol had to force herself out of her apartment before the reading. She’d spent an hour pacing a hole into her floorboards trying to work herself up to go until she just had to do it. She barely even grabbed her jacket before she was out the door, knowing that if she didn’t she would never go at all.

    She had bounded down the stairs of her building and taken a relieved breath in, as the crisp fall air hit the back of her throat. She let the cold hold her in its grasp and pull her onto the sidewalk. She let the cold make her shake rather than her fear and she pushed off into the evening.

    Carol had refused to hire a new manager to help her with her readings, she couldn’t bear the thought of looking out over a crowd and seeing a reassuring smile that wasn’t her wife's. She refused to do a book tour too, it was Number 1 on the Best Sellers List, who else was there to even buy the book? She didn't see why she should be on the road, alone, for months at a time. She wanted to be here, walking around the city she was still learning, and disappearing into crowds. She had agreed to do readings around the city as a compromise but nowhere else. The book had already made everyone so much money, the publishers had no room to argue.

    She regrets agreeing to the readings now, she regrets not agreeing to a manager now. She’s never gone to a reading alone, she doesn’t know what to say when she gets there or if there will be markers she can use already there to sign her books. What if they don’t have a copy of her book she can use to read? Can she get it on her phone?

    She pulls her phone out of her pocket and tries to buy the ebook version but it keeps giving her error messages. She stops at a crosswalk and tries to take a series of deep breaths to calm herself down. She lets her eyes slip closed and listens to the cars and the hum of people talking around her. She feels lonely. She closes the tabs on her phone and decides to call Manny. She needs something to do on this walk or she’s going to drive herself crazy and stumble through the reading. The phone rings for a minute before, finally, he answers.

    “¿Qué?” He barks into the phone.

    “Wow,” Carol drawls out. “A real warm greeting there.” She can feel him rolling his eyes through the phone.

    “Carol Sturka.”

    “Manousos Oviedo,” She parrots, the mocking calming her down already as the walk sign changes.

    “What do you want?” He demands.

    “I can’t just call you to catch up?” She asks, being purposefully obtuse.

    “It is 9:30 at night, I am going to sleep.” He grumbles.

    “God, you're old,” Carol says. He’s silent for a moment after that, he loves to do this to her. He loves to go silent and wait until she can’t take the quiet anymore. Wait until she is forced to talk before the absence of sound makes her go crazy. Carol huffs out a breath and tries to hold out for once, she gives up after 10 seconds.

    “I’m going to my reading,” she says bitterly.

    “Hmm.” He hums into the phone. Carol narrows her eyes, that's it?

    “That’s it? ‘Hmmm’?” She says in disbelief.

    “The book is good, everyone loves the book,” he said it like it was obvious. Carol smiles, letting the smug feeling seep in and soothe her, just a little.





    When she had finished the book she’d sent the manuscript to Manousos first. She called him two days later; desperate to hear what he thought of it. When he finally picked up the phone, she had started pestering him with questions.

    “You are so needy,” He had said, whining.

    “Shut up.” Carol had said with a smile, “What did you think?”

    He was quiet for a minute, Carol’s nerves had spiked in anticipation. “It is very good,” his voice was warm, gentle. “She’d like it, I think.”

    Carol had refused to ask him who he was talking about.





    “I haven’t done one of these since before,” Carol says, her voice embarrassed. “I need a distraction or maybe a pep talk,” she admits, annoyed with herself.

    They call each other like this often, Manousos had gone home not long after things started going back to normal but they stayed in touch. They called and texted regularly, neither of them was able to shake the bond they had forged or how they had come to depend on one another. Half of their calls were them arguing needlessly and the other half were them comforting each other through panic attacks. 

    Sometimes, people would come up to Carol in the aisles of Sprouts and say, “Hi, Carol,” in that horrible, familiar, happy tone. They wanted to thank her for saving the world, they were blissfully unaware of how it felt to be greeted so warmly by a stranger, unaware of how she was pushed back in time to the Joining. Carol would politely excuse herself, have a breakdown in her car, and call Manny to talk her down. 

    Sometimes, Manny called her in a panic, thinking he heard something in the static of the radio, he would hold the phone up to a speaker in his apartment and Carol would listen patiently with him until he realized it wasn’t anything, just feedback from a stray mic or someone tapping their fingers against a table's surface.

    Carol had forgotten what it felt like to have a friend, a true friend. Not just one she got drunk with at the golf course. 

    “I see,” he said. She rolled her eyes at his condescending tone, “You’re nervous.”

    “Mmhmmm.” She hums.

    “You are Carol Sturka! Savior of the universe! Best selling author in the world!” He says in a bold, cartoonish voice. She barks out a laugh in shock and dodges a kid walking hand in hand with his dad, “What is there to be nervous about? I assume you haven’t forgotten how to read?”

    “Dick,” she says with a smile into the phone. “Even the savior of the universe gets nervous every now and then.”

    “Not me,” he teases. She rolls her eyes. She lets the silence sit for a moment, this time he is the one who breaks it. “Just pretend you are reading to me.” She smiles softly, both touched and amused by just how sappy he can be sometimes. “You can even do your voices,” he says laughing.

    She groans, “I’ll never read to you again, you don’t appreciate the way I bring the story to life.”

    “A good book doesn't need to be brought to life,” he argues playfully, knowing the best way to settle Carol is to get her angry about something meaningless. 

    “Dick.” She says again, holding the phone in front of her face as she says it right into the mic. She can hear him laughing as she moves the phone back up to her ear. “I’m walking up to the store now,” she says, regretfully. 

    “Alright,” he says, his voice a little sad too. “Buena suerte, Carol Sturka,” Good Luck, Carol Sturka.

    She smiles, “Gracias, Manousos Oviedo.” She pulls the phone away from her ear and hangs up. She takes a deep breath, letting his words of encouragement fill her. She walks into the bookstore.





    The store is bigger than she expected when she walks. There is a table dead ahead covered in books with the label, “political histories,” drawn above it on pink paper. To the right is an even larger table covered in memoirs of feminists throughout time and shelves full of the staff picks. To the left is a counter with two people standing behind it chatting idly, Carol can’t focus on them yet, her eyes keep moving.

    Beyond the central table is a wall of shelves for fiction and non-fiction books. The shelves and the tables are all wooden and overflowing with books, giving the store a homey quality that makes her tight shoulders loosen. In the middle of the back wall are rows of chairs already filled with waiting people, in front of them is a small stage with two chairs and microphones set up in front of each of them. She averts her eyes, not ready to think about that yet either.

    She drifts her gaze back over to the counter, above it is a sign that says, “All are welcome!” in intricately drawn lettering, a rainbow above the words. Carol fights the urge to roll her eyes and tries to let herself feel embraced instead. There’s a pile of her books next to the register and she can hear the low chatter of people waiting for the reading to begin.

    The two people standing behind the counter, excitedly walk around it and approach her. One of them has a buzzcut, an eyebrow piercing, and two full sleeves of tattoos. They are wearing a black muscle tank and what looks like a kilt. The other has their hair up in a bun revealing an undercut. They wear an oversized red sweater that is falling down one shoulder, a pair tight, red plaid pants, and brown loafers. They smile at Carol warmly as they near her. 

    Carol feels a hundred years old next to them and like the most basic lesbian of all time. She’s wearing a pair of highwaisted, loose brown pants, a black puffer  jacket, a blue hat, and a matching scarf. Underneath the jacket, she wears a thick, cable knit, white sweater. Her hair is falling in natural waves just above her shoulders and her face is bare, she’s wearing a pair of Helen’s boots like a safety blanket.

    “Carol!” The person in the kilt said as they extended a hand to her, “I’m Ray we spoke on the phone a few days ago.” Carol takes their hand with a smile, trying to hide her returning nerves. 

    “Nice to put a face to the name,” Carol says. Her eyes move over to the person beside Ray who watches with barely contained enthusiasm. They take Carol's outstretched hand in both of theirs and hold it tenderly.

    “I’m Becca, she/her pronouns and a huge fan.” She draws out the word ‘huge’ and raises an eyebrow suggestively as she releases Carol's hand. Carol smiles back politely; she still isn't used to gay people approaching her. It was very rare that one of her readers wasn’t straight during the Wycaro era. Now when Carol is stopped because someone recognizes her it's either for saving the world or for being the woman who wrote the ‘tragic, doomed, lesbian romance book,’ she isn’t sure which one is more jarring. 

    “Thank you,” Carol says, pressing her lips together in a smile, “I’m glad Hi, Alice spoke to you.” Carol undoes her jacket, uncomfortable with the prolonged eye contact.

    “Oh, no,” Becca says, Carol puts her jacket over her arm and looks back up confused. “I mean yes! Obviously, I love Hi, Alice but I meant Wycaro, I’m such a big fan of the series.”

    “She never shuts up about it,” Ray says, conspiratorially. Carol tries to smile through her grimace at the mention of Wycaro.

    “I am seriously in love with Raban, it was very confusing for a while,” Becca says, looking at Ray and laughing, “Of course, until we all found out…” She turns to Carol and cuts herself off abruptly. “Sorry, Carol.” She says it in that same soft way, they always did, where you can’t tell if they mean it or they just know it's what you want to hear. Carol forces her body not to flinch.

    This happens sometimes, she’ll be having a conversation with a stranger, she’ll be checking out at the grocery store, she’ll be picking up dry cleaning, or she'll be walking down the street and someone will call out to her like they know her and then back away confused.

    The person checking out her groceries will pick up an item and say, “Huh, trying out grapefruits again, Carol?” They’ll apologize quickly and move on, embarrassed, and Carol will spend the rest of the night staring out of her blinds waiting for the people on the street to start seizing. No one knows why some people remember things and some don’t. No one knows why everyone seems to know everything about Carol still.

    “It’s okay.” Carol says, not wanting to dwell on it, not wanting to hear their explanation. She pulls off her hat, hoping her hair doesn’t look insane, she tries to run her fingers through it. Ray and Becca are having some kind of silent conversation, their eyes widening and their heads nodding over at Carol in panic, clearly worried they had offended her. “It’s really fine, I’m glad you liked the books, that Raban,” Carol says the name in a leading voice, “Could transcend gender.” They both laugh and their faces relax. Carol tries not to feel guilty at the name.

    “Well, now that Becca is done humiliating us,” a sharp look is sent in Becca’s direction, “Everyone is waiting patiently for you,” They gesture for her to follow them. Becca reaches out their hands, taking Carol’s coat, hat and scarf.

    “Uh, do you have a copy of the book I could borrow?” Carol asks nervously, they look to each other and then to Carol in confusion. 

    “Sure,” Ray says uncertainly, they grab a copy off the counter and pass it over to her. 

    “Thanks,” Carol says, gesturing for them to keep walking. 

    When they walk deeper into the room Carol can see there's a small crowd of maybe 30 people. They are all talking amongst themselves, some with copies of the books on their laps, some with to-go coffee cups in their hands. A few people look up as Carol walks closer and make eye contact with her before their eyes quickly dart away and then widen in excitement at their friend next to them. Carol hides her amusement by dropping her head.

    Ray guides her up onto the small stage at the front of the room, they take the furthest seat and Carol settles into the other. She grabs the bottle of water next to the chair and takes a sip as she looks out over the crowd. Eager faces, bodies sitting up straighter, legs crossing, and a floor littered in tote bags. 

    “Welcome everyone,” Ray says warmly, “Thank you all for coming out tonight. We are delighted to host best-selling author Carol Sturka and hear an excerpt from her new book, Hi, Alice.” A polite round of applause sounds out from the crowd. “We’re going to let Carol jump right in and then we’ll have a little discussion about the book and open it up to everyone for questions. Sound good?” Ray asks eagerly.

    Polite nods of heads and encouraging smiles from the crowd respond. Carol can’t help but look over their heads, her eyes seeking out Helen. She jerks back to the book in her lap when she can’t find her.

    Carol opens the book and starts flipping through it, she stops on page 218. “Alright,” she says and clears her throat, she looks out into the audience nervously, “I’ll just get right into it then.” She laughs nervously, Carol pretends it’s just her and Manousos in the room, takes a deep breath, and reads. 





    “Thank you,” Carol says, avoiding eye contact as the crowd applauds. “Thank you,” She closes the book and sets it on the table beside her, picking up her water and taking a sip. 

    “Thank you so much, for those beautiful words, Carol.” Ray says as the applause dies down. “Such an intense moment you pulled out between Alice and Yara, can you tell us why you chose that section? What about it speaks to you?”

    “Uh, well,” Carol clears her throat and shifts in her seat. “It's the first crack in the facade right?” She looks out at the crowd using their nods as fuel. “For weeks now it’s been this weird mix of avoidance and desperation, and Alice is confronted with the fact that this woman she has started to let herself fall in love with is not her wife, she may have her memories but she also had this whole life before Alice. This whole world exists inside her that Alice knows nothing about; that Yara is supposed to know nothing about.” Carol licks her lips, finding the words, she crosses her legs, “But here is this memory that Alice can’t pretend she hasn’t heard, a memory she knows isn’t her wife's, and this is the beginning of that tension, that torturing, nagging voice in the back of Alice’s head telling her that something isn’t right.” Carol looks out over the crowd, letting the words linger in the air for a moment. “It’s the major turning point in the book, when she’s forced to stop pretending… or at least when she should have stopped pretending.” Carol grimaces at the words and the crowd, thinking she is joking, laughs.

    Ray smiles and crosses their legs, mirroring Carol, they lean back in their chair. “The book is told exclusively through Alice’s perspective, did you ever consider including a glimpse into Yara’s head?”

    “No, never.” Carol says, shaking her head.

    “Can I ask why that is?” Ray pushes, “She’s such a presence throughout the whole thing but it’s almost like you can never fully pin her down. She’s such an elusive character who really changes so much over the course of the book, I would be fascinated to hear what that character is thinking and what that character feels about Alice.”

    “Of course,” Carol says, diplomatically. “The easy answer is, I have no clue what that character is feeling or thinking.” A chuckle goes around the room. God, Carol thinks, I really need to stop writing books about my life. “I wrote her to be, yes, elusive and ever evolving and changing, because she is there to make Alice feel better. She changes with the mood of the room because that’s what she is made to do. I have no clue what a person with such a strict mode of existence thinks about, let alone how they feel about that existence.” Carol pauses, the weight of the words holding her in place. “And the book isn’t meant to ask those questions necessarily. It’s meant to make you think, make all of us think, what is love? What does it mean to love someone and lose them? What does it mean if that person comes back but they’re different? Is that love still there or does it become something else?” Carol takes a deep breath and leans back in her seat. Ray smiles at her gently and turns to the crowd opening it up for questions.





    When the reading is over Carol sits at a small table by the counter and people line up to get their books signed. They lean over the table and tell her how much the book meant to them and she lets herself feel it. She lets herself feel happy that they liked the book, that it made them feel better about their lives or less alone in their pain. She lets herself get into discussions with them about the book. She lets herself be excited by the book and by the people who read it. A few people also ask her to sign Wycaro books and she lets herself enjoy that too. She lets herself laugh, without malice, at the absurdity of the book and the fact that it means so much to her. 

    She finishes signing and she stays behind to talk with a group of people who are lingering. They tell Carol about how their book club is just about to start reading Hi, Alice. How they have all read it but they need more people to talk about the book with. Carol smiles and thinks about when she first met Helen and she told Carol that her books had this rare ability to get people to want to sit down and talk to each other about them. She wishes Helen could be here for this book, Carol thinks Helen would genuinely like this one.

    Ray and Becca bring Carol her coat as she talks with the group. Carol slowly piles her layers back on as one of them asks her if she has any plans for a sequel. Carol smiles and says, “No, not right now at least.” They all groan dramatically. Carol rolls her eyes at them playfully, 542 pages and it still isn’t enough, she tugs her hat on. “Never say never though right?” 

    Before they leave, Carol passes one of them her card and tells them to call her if they’d be interested in her coming to talk to their book club. The person's eyes light up and they say they will absolutely call. Carol tries to hide how happy it makes her but then decides, fuck it, and smiles back at them. She waves to the group as they leave and turns back to Ray and Becca. They had been standing a few feet away, waiting patiently to speak to her.

    “Is everything okay?” Carol asked.

    "Absolutely!” Ray said, “it's just…” They point down to Carol’s arms. She still has the copy of the book she borrowed.

    “Shit,” Carol passes it back over to them, “Sorry about that, I totally forgot.”

    “Don’t worry about it, it just saves us from having to recount the inventory,” They chuckle, Carol nods awkwardly, she wishes Manny was in town so they could go to dinner or something, she isn’t quite ready to go home yet. She wants to stay in this feeling longer.

    “Of course,” Carol says, stalling, they look at her uncertainly. “Thank you again for having me, it was a pleasure.” She smiles and turns to go with a sinking feeling.

    “Wait!” Becca calls out, Carol turns back, hopeful. “I almost forgot,” she runs around the counter and comes back with a book in her hands. Becca passes it and a purple sharpie over to Carol. Winds of Wycaro, the first book in the series. Carol smiles as she takes it from her. “Do you mind?” Becca asks sheepishly.

    “Of course not,” Carol flips it open to the title page and writes. “To Becca, may you find your very own Raban,” and hands it back over. Becca reads the inscription and hugs it to her chest, her body shaking in excitement. 

    “Thanks, Carol.” She says with a wide smile. Carol smiles back and finally pushes out of the bookstore after a gentle wave goodbye. The cold wind hits her and she zips her jacket higher. 

    She pushes her hands into her pockets and tries to think of somewhere to go, she could call Manny again but it's midnight there now and she doesn't want to put up with his grumbling. She wants to tell him about the reading, how good it felt, how easy it felt. How she missed Helen the whole time and she kept pausing during the reading to look at her boots. How it made her simultaneously feel less alone and so goddamn lonely.

    She's going to walk home, it’s about an hour's distance from here but her jacket is warm and her body is still flooded with adrenaline from the reading and she needs to be in the world right now. She isn’t ready to be still yet, she isn’t ready to be alone again. Carol turns and starts down the sidewalk, she gets about ten feet from the bookstore when her steps stutter and she comes to an abrupt halt.

    She’s there.

    Pacing back and forth on the corner of the block.

    The real version. The version Carol hasn't seen in twenty years.

    Her fingers are wrapped around a cigarette and there’s a cloud of smoke following after her, she’s muttering something under her breath that Carol can’t quite make out. Carol brings a hand up to rub at her eyes, thinking she’s seeing things, it wouldn’t be the first time. When Carol brings her hand down, she's still there.

    She looks like herself again, the difference is so shocking to Carol she can’t even imagine how she got used to the suits and the dresses and the lipstick. How she had let herself forget what she really looked like, how she had let herself pretend.

    Her hair is cut short, falling just under her chin, it’s tucked behind her ear on the left side and falling in waves on the other. She’s wearing a dark grey, hooded Carhartt jacket that looks worn in. Carol can see a tear in the sleeve from how close she is. She keeps pacing, drawing Carol's eyes down to her legs, covered in a pair of loose straight leg jeans. Carol thinks she might cry at the sight of them and she forces down a whimper. 

    She throws her cigarette on the ground and stomps it out on the pavement, her feet covered in a beat up pair of Doc Martens, her pant legs are rolled up over the ankles. Carol watches her as she pulls out a pack of cigarettes, brings another to her lips and lights it without stopping her stride. The cigarette bouncing in the air as her mouth moves and she continues talking to herself.

    Her shoulders are slouched forward, like they always used to be, gone is the perfect posture, the totally relaxed figure, the hand clasped over her forearm. The person in front of Carol looks wounded tight, about to explode with nervous energy. Her hands move with little switches at whatever she is saying, she brings her hands together periodically to wring them together or to hold the cigarette as she exhales. It shakes between her fingers. Carol takes a hesitant step closer, unable to resist. When Carol moves closer she can hear the whispers from under her breath a bit more clearly. 

    A steady stream of incomprehensible Polish flowing out of her mouth. Carol’s heart stutters a beat at the sound, she hadn’t realized how much she missed it. She had forgotten so much about the woman in front of her, so much of it lost to time, so much of it lost to the version she was during the Joining. To see her in front of Carol now. So alive, so singular. Carol fights the urge to cry, she fights the urge to take her into her arms and hold her as tight as she can. She fights the urge to run to her and tell her how much she has missed her, how she never thought she would see her again, how she was so sorry, how nothing Carol could say would ever make it better but she was so fucking glad to see her. 

    Carol shoves all those urges down, knowing they wouldn’t be appreciated.

    Carol watches her for a moment longer, letting herself take it in. Carol had forgotten how stunning she really was. She was beautiful during the Joining but she wasn’t like this. She looks older now, her face bare, her eyebrows look thicker than Carol remembers. Her face looks angry, she’s so wonderfully expressive like this. She even looks taller than Carol remembers her being. She looks just as Carol remembered her; tall, dark, and serious. 

    Carol doesn't know why she’s come or what she's here to say, but she wants to remember this moment. Before they have spoken, when all Carol feels when she looks at her isn’t guilt or pain or self hatred, but relief.

    She takes a deep breath and when she’s ready, she steps forward and calls out to her.

    “Zosia?”

     

     

     

     

     

     

     

    Chapter 13: Part Two: The Return

    Chapter Notes

    2029, 4 years, 7 months, 2 weeks, and 17 days since the Unjoining.

     

    “Zosia?”

     

     

    Zosia’s head jerks over to Carol at the sound of her voice. She stops pacing, her shoes scraping on the sidewalk at the sudden halt. Carol stares at her in shock, Zosia starts coughing violently around the smoke stuck in her lungs. She brings a hand up to her chest and looks away from Carol to the ground as she coughs wildly.

    Carol takes a step forward, wanting to help. Zosia holds out a hand, telling her to stop, her eyes coming back up to Carol's. She looks overwhelmed. Her eyes are full of so much emotion Carol doesn't even know where to start in decoding it. Her eyes bore into Carol, her body lighting up at the feeling of it, twenty years apart and it still remembers her.

    They stare at each other as Zosia’s coughing dies down, their eyes tracing over each other, maybe with reverence, maybe with judgement, maybe with disgust Carol can’t find it in herself to care. Zosia is in front of her. She looks amazing, she looks so angry, she looks perfect. 

    After Zosia’s coughing stops completely, Carol watches in disbelief as she immediately takes another drag. She can’t help but roll her eyes at the absurdity.

    “What?” Zosia bites out angrily at her, her first words. Her accent is different, it’s somewhere in between what it was in Berlin and what it was in the Joining. Carol loves it immediately.

    “Nothing,” she says defensively. Zosia takes another drag and rolls her eyes back at Carol. She exhales slowly through her nose as she stares. Carol had forgotten what it felt like to have Zosia’s eyes on her. How she could feel it on her skin like a caress. How deep Zosia’s eyes were, how warm they felt. How Carol felt like Zosia could see right through her when she looked at her. How Carol felt laid bare in front of Zosia every time their eyes met. 

    She remembers it all as Zosia’s eyes trace over her. Carol fights the urge to cross her arms in front of her body to hide. She swallows hard as she watches the smoke stream out of Zosia’s nose.

    Carol can smell the smoke from where she stands, it brings her back to Berlin, leaning against a wall next to Zosia as she smoked, watching her with a fond smile and Zosia holding her cigarette up to Carol’s lips. Zosia’s fingers would brush against the sensitive skin of Carol’s mouth as she did. It would make Carol’s lips tingle and Zosia would stare at her with this knowing look and then spend the rest of the night with her hand dangerously high on Carol’s thigh.

    She can feel the smoke filling the air around them on this street corner, she can feel Zosia’s appraising eyes running over her just how they used to. Carol stays quiet, unsure if she would be able to speak with how dry her mouth feels.

    Zosia breaks the silence first, “You wrote another book about me.” Jesus.

    Carol has suffered a lot of humiliation over the course of her life but this, this, is the most embarrassed she has ever felt. She can feel her face flushing at the words. She presses her lips together in a pained smile and raises her eyebrows. Her head bounces up and down as she nods, processing what was just said. She looks away from Zosia.

    Carol shoves her hands into her back pockets, trying to appear unaffected. “Yep,” she says the ‘p’ popping, her voice breathy. 

    Zosia rolls her eyes again and throws the butt in her hands to the ground and crushes it under her foot, she stares down at it as she does. Her face losing some of its anger, her jaw clenches and unclenches as she twists her foot over the cigarette. She looks like she’s fighting some internal battle and losing. When she finally looks up at Carol her eyes are almost tender, just for a moment, before it disappears. Zosia takes a step closer to Carol, she pushes her hands into her jacket's pockets and leans her head down slightly to bring their eyes to the same level.

    “I am sorry about Helen.” Carol feels her lungs deflate at the words, she crosses her arms in front of her chest and tries to hide how hard they are wrapping around her body, how they are the only thing keeping her upright. The last words Carol was expecting to hear. “She loved you very much,” Zosia looks away as she speaks, her face pained.

    Carol tries with everything she has to swallow the emotion down that is welling up inside her but a single tear slips through. She looks away to the street, hiding it from Zosia. Carol wipes it away quickly, “Thank you,” Carol says curtly. Not ready to have a conversation about Helen, especially not with Zosia. “Why are you…” Carol trails off not wanting to be rude, but she doesn’t understand what is happening. “Why are you here?” She asks, and feels guilty, worried that Zosia will think she doesn’t want to see her. “I mean, it’s fine that you are,” Carol insists. “Obviously, you can go anywhere you want, see anyone you want,” Carol cringes at the words, as if she has any control over what Zosia does. “I know you hate me. I get it really, after everything in…” she can’t say that. “And while you were…” she can’t say that either. Carol clears her throat to get rid of the words, to try to find the right ones. “I just figured I would never see you again.” She narrows her eyes at herself, feeling presumptuous. “Not that I deserve to see you again, that is totally your choice and I totally respect that, I—”

    “Stop talking.” Zosia interrupts her. Carol takes a deep breath, glad to no longer be speaking. “I live here.” Zosia answers. Carol had forgotten how little she actually spoke. She almost smiles at the curt sentence, the corners of her mouth curling up without her control. 

    “You live here?” Carol asks, raising an eyebrow, trying to draw out a longer answer from Zosia just like she used to, both of them swallow hard at the familiar feeling. 

    “Yes. I live here,” Zosia says like she’s annoyed, “In Chicago,” she adds it like that gives Carol all the information she needs. God, she had forgotten how annoying it was how little Zosia spoke.

    “How long have you lived here?” Carol asks, forgetting who they are to each other now, just feeling annoyed.

    “None of your business,” Zosia says harshly. Carol flushes again, ashamed of herself for asking. 

    “Sorry, I didn't mean to…” Carol trails off gesturing between them, unable to find the right words to apologize. 

    Zosia smiles as she watches Carol flounder. “I’m joking,” Zosia chuckles. “I lived here for two years, before,” she stops herself, her eyes running over Carol from head to toe, “You know.”

    Carol nods and smiles uncomfortably, unsure what she should say to that. “I thought you were in Morocco when it happened?” Carol asks because she apparently can’t control herself.

    Zosia’s face darkens and she clenches her jaw. “Yes,” she forces out. “I was,” Zosia takes a deep breath, “I was on holiday.” 

    What is happening? Carol doesn't know what to do, she doesn't know how to stand, she doesn’t know where to look, she doesn't know what to say. She doesn't know why Zosia is here or what she wants. Why was she out here waiting? Carol can feel herself starting to shut down, she wants to leave, she isn’t ready for this. She isn’t ready to face Zosia.

    She needs to pull herself together, Zosia is still watching her and she isn’t hiding it well enough how much her presence is affecting her.

    “I thought we should talk,” Zosia says, her voice almost embarrassed. She pulls her pack back out.

    “Why now?” Carol asks, confused as she watches Zosia bring a lighter up to her mouth.

    Zosia lights another cigarette and takes a drag, her eyes closing as she breathes out, her chest rising with the force of it, maybe she isn't handling this that well either. “I walked past earlier,” Zosia says, pointing somewhere over Carol’s shoulder, Carol follows the glow of the cigarette cherry and looks over her shoulder. There’s a sandwich board outside the bookstore that reads: 

    “A reading and Q+A with Carol Sturka, Author of the Winds of Wycaro series about her new book, Hi, Alice. Tonight at 7pm!”

    Carol lets her head turn back to Zosia, her eyes narrowed in confusion, “And you waited out here for me?” Her voice is breathy.

    Zosia shrugs and takes another drag, when she speaks it’s almost a whisper, “Old habits.” She says it with her eyes on the ground, unable to meet Carol’s. Carol has the overwhelming urge to grab Zosia’s face in her hands, pull it down to her shoulder, wrap her arms around her as tight as she can and hold her. 

    Instead, she stays exactly where she is, her feet feel rooted to the ground. Carol tries to find something to say that will make things better, that will make things easier for the both of them, but she can’t think of anything. She never was good at comforting Zosia. She never even tried.

    A group of people walks by them, shouting and jumping on each other as they do. The interruption reminds Carol that they are in the middle of a sidewalk and that it is freezing. She pulls her jackets tighter around her, feeling the cold for the first time since she walked out of the bookstore.

    Carol gathers all her strength and asks, “Do you want to get a coffee or something?” Her voice, unsure.

    Zosia stares at her while she inhales deeply. “It’s 9 pm,” she says dismissively as she exhales. Carol looks away dejected. Zosia purses her lips, “A drink?” She says and Carol hears it for the olive branch it is.

    Carol’s eyes close, embarrassed once more, she turns back to Zosia, opens her eyes and swallows heavily. “I don’t drink anymore,” Carol says, feeling small. 

    Zosia looks surprised but she hides it pretty well, just a raise of her eyebrow. Carol can tell she wants to ask more about that but she doesn’t. “Good for you,” Zosia says, like she means it and Carol tries to pretend her pulse doesn’t race at the word ‘good’ coming out of Zosia’s mouth. “A non-alcoholic drink then?” Zosia asks playfully, her accent heavy around the words, enunciating every syllable. 





    The walk is silent after Carol agrees. It almost feels like old times. Zosia leads Carol down the street, chainsmoking as they go. Carol feels the contrast between then and now more fully as they walk. Zosia in Berlin would be holding Carol’s hand or wrapping her arm around Carol’s waist. Her head would be turned toward Carol at an angle that couldn’t possibly be comfortable but that she would never complain about. Zosia from then would be watching her with that gentle look, cataloging every thing that Carol’s eyes lingered on to see if anything on the street made her happy. She would walk next to her like she was floating, her steps barely making a sound, and Carol would reach out a hand to her. 

    Zosia now has her hands deep in her pockets, her eyes stay firmly forward, and her neck is stiff. Almost like she is working very hard not to look over at Carol, but that couldn’t be true.

    When they stop after a short walk, Zosia directs her into a bar. Zosia holds the door and lifts her hand to press against Carol’s back to guide her in, when Zosia realizes what she was about to do she jerks her hand away. Carol hears her mutter something angrily under her breath in Polish at herself as she flicks her cigarette out the door. Carol makes a note to look it up later, “Żałosne.” Pathetic.

    The bar was probably half full, with people scattered along the stools and a few groups at tables chatting. Carol can see a pool table at the back of the long room. Over the bar are some multicolored lights that glow off of the glasses below. The music is low enough to hear over, but loud enough you could comfortably sit in silence. 

    Carol pulls her hat off to distract herself as her eyes linger on the shelves behind the bar. She turns back to Zosia, who stands over her shoulder, already watching Carol. Their eyes meet for and for a moment neither of them breathes. She’s so close. With the door closing behind them and Carol freezing in the entryway, Zosia is nearly pressed up against Carol’s back. Carol can feel their jackets brushing against each other as if her raw nerves are exposed to the air.

    Carol’s hands freeze in front of her where she holds her hat. Zosia’s eyes trail over her face, Carol feels the path they take like fingertips against her skin. Tracing over the contours of her face, noting all the changes and details. Carol watches Zosia’s eyes as they move over her. She looks… sad? Zosia’s eyes always sparkle; it's what makes them so mesmerizing but Carol swears they are glistening with something deeper. 

    A loud noise jars Zosia out of her staring, her eyes jump to the noise. Someone in the back of the bar has won a game of pool and is celebrating their victory very loudly. Carol clears her throat, uncertain what to do next. She feels Zosia lift an arm behind her and looks back in shock, for a moment she thinks Zosia is going to touch her, but her hand goes by Carol’s body and she points to a table in a quiet corner of the room. She steps around Carol and makes her way to the bar without a word. 

    Carol takes a settling breath as she leaves and walks over to the table Zosia pointed at. She sets her hat and her scarf on one of the empty seats and wonders if she should take her coat off or not. She decides not to, unsure how long they will be here and worried about being too exposed in front of Zosia’s watchful eyes. She lets her roam over the bar, the photos on the wall, the people sitting on stools until her eyes naturally find Zosia. She’s already looking at Carol. 

    For a moment, when their eyes meet, Zosia looks at her just how she used to. Like they are the only two people in the room and her eyes can’t help themselves but seek Carol out, unable to spend a moment not looking at her.

    But she drops her head and looks away, and Carol is left to wonder if she imagined it. Carol swallows down the hurt again and forces her eyes away. She looks down at the surface of the table and traces a finger over the cracks in its surface. Trying to calm her breathing and slow her racing heart before Zosia returns. 

    Carol jumps when a glass is set down in front of her, Zosia takes a seat across from her at the table with a matching drink. Carol leans forward and takes a sip from the straw. Diet fucking Pepsi. 

    “Seriously?” Carol says angrily. Zosia just smiles smugly around the rim of her own drink, her straw on the table in front of her. Carol looks away shaking her head, trying to keep her temper from flaring. She can’t get angry at Zosia. Not after everything.

    Carol still isn’t used to being allowed to be angry again. Everytime Carol so much as raises her voice she sees a vision of Zosia convulsion against the ground in front of her and the anger quickly fades to guilt.

    Carol turns away to watch a group two tables away, four women sit around the table, probably Zosia’s age. They look like they are catching up, gasping in shock at each other's stories and laughing as they interrupt one another. Carol watches them and wonders what it's like to just have an easy night out with friends. She can hardly remember how it felt anymore.

    She turns back to Zosia, feeling her stare on her face. She glares at the younger woman, tired of being examined. Zosia isn’t bothered by Carol's glare, she keeps staring, just watching her. Carol shifts uncomfortably around in her chair. Ding!

    Zosia breaks eye contact with Carol and pulls her phone out of her jacket’s inside pocket, she starts typing something very quickly. Carol waits for a moment, her phone dings again, she smiles and types something back. She takes a sip of her drink and her phone dings again. Carol tries not to interrupt her, tries not to be nosy, but Jesus Christ, how rude?

    “Everything alright?” Carol asks eventually. 

    “Yes,” Zosia says, putting the phone back into her pocket, “Sorry.” She takes another sip of her Pepsi, as she sets down the glass she says, “Miles says hello.”

    Carol almost doesn't understand what she is saying for a moment, “Miles?” Carol says in shock, “Miles, Miles?” 

    “Yes,” Zosia says with a smile, “Miles Miles,” her voice both mocking and amused. “He followed me to Chicago when I got my job.”

    “What job?” Carol asks, feeling crazed.

    “Professor of history and gender sexuality,” Zosia says with a blank face.

    “You’re a professor?” Carol asks, shocked. She leans forward her finger pointing into the surface of the table toward Zosia.

    “Yes,” Zosia says, with an annoyed nod of her head. “So is Miles.” She takes a sip of her drink and looks away. “He teaches creative writing,” she turns back to Carol with a smug smile. “He likes your books.”

    Carol feels her anger boiling up beyond the point of return, she feels humiliated that she didn’t know what Zosia did, that she was a professor and Carol had no clue she had even gone to college. She feels angry that Miles has gotten to spend twenty years with Zosia and she feels angry that Zosia has friends and she doesn’t. She feels angry that Zosia has now thrown her books in Carol's face twice and that Carol has just had to take it. She wants Zosia to feel the way she is feeling. She wants to stop feeling so guilty.

    “Does Miles like your tattoo?” Carol bites back smugly as the surprise hits Zosia’s face. Carol reveals in her moment of control. Until she sees the hurt on Zosia’s face, the embarrassment. Carol watches Zosia bring a hand up to the back of her neck and rub over the mark unconsciously. 

    Carol swallows down the wave of disgust she feels toward herself. “Look, can we just get to you throwing your drink in my face and telling me off?” Carol says angrily, to hide how much she hates herself right now. “I violated you, I get it,” Zosia stiffens but Carol doesn’t notice in her anger. “But can we stop pretending like this is normal? Like we are just two gals catching up?” Carol says sarcastically, unable to continue on like this. 

    Zosia’s jaw clenches, the muscles in her face jumping at the action. “I thought you liked pretending?” She says, her eyes staring hard into Carol’s. Whose fall into her lap, there’s that metaphorical drink to the face.

    “You didn’t…” Zosia says, her voice softer now, regretful. “I don’t know what you did but it wasn’t that.” Zosia says, waiting for Carol to meet her eyes again before she continues. “I wasn’t, not there, If I was I would have—” She cuts herself off, now Zosia looks down at her lap, and says that same word in Polish, “Żałosne,” Pathetic. She takes a sip of her drink and finishes quietly, “It wasn’t that.”

    Carol lets herself feel better for a moment, knowing that later, the crushing shame will return but grateful for the momentary reprieve. “Why did you stop outside the store?” Carol asks after they are both silent for a moment, her voice tired.

    “I thought we should talk,” Zosia says again. 

    “Talk about what?” Carol asks, growing annoyed again.

    “To start?” Zosia leans forward, her voice annoyed too. “Berlin.” The word hangs in the air between them. Carol squirms in her seat, trying not to look away. Tchórz. 

    “Don’t you already know everything?” Carol bites out, her body almost shaking with how uncomfortable she feels. “Helen knew it all.”

    Zosia clenches her jaw again, holding herself back. “I’d rather not rely on your dead wife’s memories,” Zosia says harshly. “As I’m sure you can understand, it's quite uncomfortable.” Zosia spits out, her voice cracking at the end. Carol watches her body slump over slightly, she notices the bags under Zosia’s eyes for the first time and watches her with softer eyes.

    “Do you remember everything?” Carol asks after a moment of silence, scared of the question, even more scared of the answer.

    Zosia doesn’t reply immediately, her hands wrap around the glass, her rings clinking against it as she taps a finger against the surface. “From Helen or from during?” Zosia asks finally. Carol just shakes her head, unable to answer, unsure which one she was even asking about.

    Zosia continues anyway. “Most things, not everything,” she pauses, staring at the cup in front of her, with blank eyes as she speaks. “Everything was boring,” she draws out the word, her eyes widening. “And then you would try to kill me with a hand grenade." Her eyes jumped back up to Carol's, “These things were a bit more memorable.” 

    “You gave me the grenade,” Carol says defensively, Zosia flinches. “It gave me the grenade!” Carol tries to correct herself again, “She gave me the…” Carol closes her eyes and her shoulders drop, there’s nothing to say. “I’m sorry,” Carol looks back up at Zosia. “I didn’t know it was real,” she says, her voice defeated.

    “I know,” Zosia says after a moment. “It doesn't matter anyway,” she lifts her drink to her lips and smiles around it, “Girls like scars.”

    Carol is going to leave. She is going to leave and never come back to this bar. Maybe she will even leave this city. 

    Zosia laughs loudly as she watches Carol's face reel up in disgust. Carol blushes and rolls her eyes. “Asshole,” Carol mutters, taking a drink from the forgotten glass in front of her. She dreads where this conversation is going to go next. 

    “Should we talk about Raban instead?” Zosia asks, her voice oh-so-innocent. Carol spits the Pepsi back into the glass. She coughs around the liquid caught in her throat. Zosia smiles, amused and passes her a napkin between two fingers. 

    Carol takes it angrily, but still making sure she doesn’t touch Zosia’s hand, she wipes her mouth praying that Zosia is joking. She’d rather talk about Berlin or the grenade or how Zosia still has all her dead wife’s memories than about Raban. 

    “I read it when it came out,” Zosia says as Carol slowly lowers the napkin from her face. “Miles found it in a bookshop and mailed it to me.” Fucking Miles, Carol thinks. Zosia leans forward, her forearms on the table's surface. “At first I was angry,” she pauses. “I mean the cover was insane,” she stares at Carol with searching eyes.

    Carol flushes and closes her eyes, “I know,” she chokes out. 

    “Then I was hurt,” Zosia scoffs. “I mean, a man? Really, Carol?” Both of them flinch at her use of Carol's name, Zosia clearly didn’t mean to say it. She shakes her head and pushes on. “Then I was confused.” She licks her lips, “If you loved me so much,” her voice wavers, “If you loved me so much,” Her voice is a little firmer this time, “Why would you leave?”

    Carol stares back, all the time she had spent thinking about what it would be like if Zosia ever read her books was not enough to prepare her for the reality of this conversation. She swallows and tries to think of anything she can say, “I wasn’t ready,” Carol tries. The same line she has used for years to make herself feel better about abandoning Zosia.

    The woman across from her slams a hand into the surface of the table, making their glasses shake and Carol jump. She points a finger at Carol, “No.” She shakes her head, “Don’t give me that.”

    Carol holds her gaze for a moment, fighting her instincts to get out of this conversation as fast as she can, “I was scared,” she says after a minute. Zosia leans back slightly, letting Carol continue. “I didn’t know how to deal with it all,” She pauses, delaying. “I didn’t know how to deal with you and I was scared.” She knows what Zosia wants to hear, “I was a coward.” She finishes, forcing herself to hold eye contact. 

    She watches a hundred emotions ripple over the surface of Zosia’s face, anger, relief, love, pride, heartbreak, before it settles on something…pained. “What was so much better about her?” Zosia asks finally, her voice broken and Carol’s heart stops beating. “What did she have that I didn’t?”

    Carol doesn’t have the slightest clue how to answer that. How to even begin to try and answer that. She feels her stomach turn at the idea of Zosia having twenty years of memories of Carol being happy and in love with someone else. Twenty years of Carol letting a person in when she wouldn’t even ask Zosia what her job was when they were together. 

    Carol’s eyes widen in shock as she struggles to find an answer. Zosia waits across from her growing more and more agitated. “It was…” No. “After…” Wrong. “You…” Definitely not. What she does say is worse than any of the others, “Nothing. She wasn’t better or worse. It was different. I was different.” She paused, “After… Berlin, I promised myself I wouldn’t do that again, not after I saw the way you looked at me when I left.” She stops there hoping that Zosia can hear what she means, that she regrets it, that she loved her, that she loved Helen, that she was too much of a coward to try to fix things with Zosia, how she made Carol want to be better for the next person.

    Zosia scoffs, and looks away, Carol sees her eyes well up with unshed tears. She turns back to Carol, “I am so glad that I could open you up to love.” She spits at Carol, her voice rising with every word. “I’m so glad that my broken heart gave you twenty years with the love of your life.” Zosia shouts at her; she jumps up out of her chair and storms out of the bar. 

    This time, Carol watches as she goes.

     

     

     

     

    Chapter End Notes

    New chapter a week from today at the latest!

    Chapter 14: Part Two: The Hard Truth

    Chapter Notes

    probably rewrote this chapter at least 5 times this week, enjoy :)

    Carol sits there for a while after Zosia leaves, trying to make sense of what her life has become. The last 15 minutes, the last 30 minutes, the last hour, the whole night, the last four years; none of it has felt real. She doesn't know how to handle seeing Zosia again or Zosia’s anger. She had spent so long preparing for Zosia’s anger but Carol thought that the books and Berlin and everything that happened there would pale in comparison to their lives during the Joining.

     

    She had expected Zosia’s anger to be about the joining, not… Helen. 

     

    What was so much better about her?

     

    Carol had promised herself, after everything was over, that she would not seek Zosia out, she would not try to find Zosia after the unjoining. When Zosia didn’t show up in the first few months Carol hadn’t been surprised. People were returning home, the world's governments were slowly taking back control of the people and there were food shortages around the globe. Carol figured Zosia was stuck, wherever she was, just like everyone else.

     

    But then a year passed and Carol still didn’t hear anything.

     

    After a year and a half she had finally started to accept she would never see Zosia again.

     

    But then she was outside the bookstore. 

     

    Carol knew she had no right to ask anything of Zosia, let alone forgiveness. Carol had already spent too much of their relationship thinking only of herself. But she just didn’t understand Zosia’s anger.

     

    She expected Zosia to be angry about being used as Carol’s ‘chaperone,’ she expected anger about Carol’s relationship with her. She didn’t understand why Zosia didn’t even seem to care about these things, she had barely even brought up the joining tonight. Maybe it’s just easier to be angry about Berlin? The circumstances of that situation were much less complicated than whatever was happening during the joining. Maybe Zosia is just as confused by it as Carol? Maybe she doesn’t know how to put what happened between them into words either.

     

    Sometimes; Carol thinks about her. About the version of Zosia she was sent.

     

    Sometimes, Carol wonders if it hurt when they were all disconnected. Sometimes Carol wonders if that person is still inside this Zosia. Sometimes, Carol wonders if she loved her because she was Zosia or because she wasn’t. Because she had her face and her memories but none of the anger and resentment. Because it was easy to love Zosia without all the complications, without having to face what Carol had done to her.





    Carol walks home from the bar in a haze, she welcomes the numbing effect of the cold on her mind, unable to think about anything other than getting home and getting inside. She walks without thinking, letting the muscle memory guide her.

     

    Carol moved to Chicago a few months ago, she had chosen the city, almost at random. She remembered coming over the years with Helen on book tours. They always liked the city, the people, the food; Carol had liked her fans in Chicago the most. She always looked forward to coming to the city, it just happened to be the only place where almost no one asked her about Raban. 

     

    She decided to leave Albuquerque after walking around her house like a ghost for months, every room a reminder of Helen and now Zosia. Every place in the city was somewhere she had gone with one of them and or had planned to go with one of them.
