    They would sit on the concrete beach, the water lapping against the steep edge and Carol would go on long rants about how it was not a beach while Miles rolled his eyes and Zosia leaned back on her elbows with something Carol swore was affection brimming in her eyes as she watched them trade barbs. 

     

    The ‘beach’ was like a huge staircase, each step almost two feet wide and tall, before plateauing into a little platform that cut off into a tiny cliff you could jump off of into the water below. They would sit on the steps and eat while bikers, runners and swimmers would pass them by. As the weather only got warmer, more and more people would sit across the steps. Sometimes Miles would go swimming and leave Zosia and Carol alone together. 

     

    “Do you like swimming?” Carol had asked nervously one day, and cringed as the lame words had left her mouth. 

     

    Zosia was leaned back against a step, sunglasses on her face, her head was tilted back towards the sun, she had one leg outstretched and one bent up, her arms were crossed over her chest. If Carol was any less aware of Zosia she would have thought she was asleep but every now and then she could feel the weight of Zosia’s eyes on her, even through the thick lenses that covered them. 

     

    Zosia wore a white t-shirt with the sleeves pushed up around her shoulders and a pair of green Adidas basketball shorts, she had taken her shoes and socks off, and had a cigarette tucked behind her right ear. Her sunglasses were a weird remake of a 90s rectangular style with cherry red frames and dark black lenses. Zosia continued her war against bras and Carol continued to pretend she didn’t notice. 

     

    Carol wore a grey tank top, a navy blue short sleeve button down and brown linen pants. She kept her shoes on since they were a pair of sandals and very airy. She had set aside a book she brought with her in her lap before she’d asked Zosia her question. 

     

    “What?” Zosia said, turning to Carol confused, she pushed the sunglasses back on her head, pulling short strands of hair up with the motion. 

     

    “Do you like swimming?” Carol asked again, trying not to lose her nerve. 

     

    “Do I like swimming?” Zosia repeated the question slowly, like she needed to be sure that was what Carol had actually asked her. 

     

    “Mmhmm,” Carol said, her lips pressed together tightly. 

     

    “Yes, I like swimming.” Carol nods and they both turn back out to the water, watching Miles as he floats on his back, a pair of sunglasses on his face that Carol is sure he will lose before he returns to them.

     

    She hears Zosais clear her throat behind her and shift where it sits, her clothes scraping against the concrete. “Do you like swimming?” Zosia asks, her voice small.

     

    Carol keeps her head turned towards Miles as a smile spreads across her face, not wanting to startle Zosia with her happiness. She turns back when it has faded from bursting to considering. “Yes, I like swimming.” Carol swallows, “I don’t do it often but I always loved being in the water when I was a kid.” She turns back to Miles and smiles wistfully. “I wanted to join the swim team for a while but my mother refused to let me.” Carol laughs darkly, “She said there would be ‘too much temptation,’” she turns back to Zosia as she finishes, rolling her eyes. 

     

    Zosia watches her for a moment, she sits forward, bringing her closer to Carol, she doesn’t touch her, she doesn’t speak. Her index finger traces over the texture of the stone between them delicately and Carol watches her finger move, feeling her heartrate slow as it does. 

     

    “Your mother sounds like a bitch,” Zosia murmurs quietly, her head resting on her knee. Carol can hear the smile even if she can’t see it. She laughs gently in return, she leans back on the palms of her hands and raises her eyebrows.

     

    “I mean she had a point,” Carol smiles at a memory, it doesn’t feel scary to talk about it in this moment, it feels good. “My first…” Carol struggles to find the word, “girlfriend I guess,” Carol pauses letting the word settle inside her chest for a moment, “Sarah, she was on the swim team.” 

     

    Zosia raises her head so their eyes can meet and a slow proud smile spreads across her face and she scoffs at Carol in amusement. 

     

    “What about the swim team?” Miles says as he drops back down onto his spread out towel and wraps it around his hips. Zosia and Carol stare at each other for a moment longer before Zosia breaks eye contact and turns to Miles. 

     

    “Carol’s first girlfriend was on the swim team,” Zosia says, her eyebrows raising in scandal, knowing how much he’ll love that, Carol rolls her eyes in preparation.

     

    “You little minx,” He kicks his foot into Carol’s leg and leaves a wet spot on her pants that she feigns outrage at. “My first boyfriend was on the football team in school, he was painfully closeted and of course broke my heart,” Carol and Zosia both smile as he brings the back of his hand up to his forehead and drops his head back. “But every time I see a cleat I still feel a little tingle of something.” He drops his hand and closes his eyes inhaling deeply. When he opens his eyes he looks over to Carol, “Do you have that?”

    “A cleat kink?” She mocks, trying not to think about Zosia in her old jersey, “Can’t say that I do.” He kicks her again and Carol hears Zosia let out a little laugh as she lights the cigarette she had tucked behind her ear. “How about you?” Carol asks Zosia, “First girlfriend?”

     

    Zosia’s smile fades and the energy of their conversation turns stale. Zosia flicks the cigarette, some ash falling onto the step beside her. “Never had one,” Zosia says, her voice flat.

     

    Carol’s eyes furrow in shock, “Not ever before…” She turns to look at Miles who is shaking his head subtly, both answering her question and begging her to stop. Carol shuts her mouth quickly and lets Miles distract Zosia with the tale of how he lost his sunglasses in the water. Carol tries not to watch Zosia too closely after that and she tries not to speak unless spoken to. 

     

    Not even before?





    2029, 4 years, 11 months, 1 weeks, and 5 days since the Unjoining.

     

    Carol and Zosia are sitting by the water waiting for Miles to arrive in silence. Carol’s fingers tap an irregular rhythm on her knee as she waits. Zosia is sitting almost four feet away from her, staring out at the water, smoking, and ignoring her. Carol watches Zosia, wondering how a side profile can be so mesmerizing, when Zosia’s phone starts ringing. 

     

    “Halo?” Zosia says into the phone without looking at whose calling. She turns her head just an inch toward Carol as she listens. “Poważnie?” Seriously? Carol pulls her own phone out to see if Miles has texted, nothing. “Jesteś dupkiem,” You’re an asshole. Zosia hangs up the phone and drops it down onto the concrete beside her, between the two of them. “Miles can’t make it today,” Zosia says without turning her head, her voice sarcastic.

     

    Carol takes a deep breath in and lets out a disbelieving chuckle.

     

    “What?” Zosia asks, finally turning to look at Carol. 

     

    “Nothing,” Carol says. “I guess we should have expected this though, right? How long did you think he would be able to hold himself back?”

     

    Zosia is quiet for a moment, “At least another week.” She looks over with a teasing smile on her face to Carol, she knocks her finger against her cigarette and ash floats away toward the water. 

     

    It’s the first time they’ve been truly alone since the office, Miles or an Uber driver has always been with them. Carol feels like she needs to say something, thinks she should fill the air with something, anything. Maybe she should apologize again. Maybe she should ask Zosia a question again, because that went so well the last time. 

     

    Instead they sit in silence. They both pull their lunches out of their bags. Carol’s a caprese wrap and a bag of chips she got at a shop near her apartment. Zosia’s is a metal container full of what looks like a quinoa salad. They lean their backs against the step behind them and eat sitting two feet away from one another. Carol offers Zosia her chips and hides a pleased smile when the younger woman takes one. Zosia offers Carol a piece of chocolate and mutters a shy, “Sorry,” as the chocolate melts all over Carol’s fingers.

     

    “Don’t worry about it,” Carol says. She turns back to the water and sucks the chocolate off her fingers. She hums at the taste, “It's good. Where did you get it?” She asks, turning back to face Zosia who is staring at her mouth. Zosia jerks her head away, and turns back to the water. 

     

    “My mother sends them every few months.” Zosia says, flicking the wheel of her lighter in her lap, not hard enough to light it but hard enough to make a gentle clicking noise rhythmically sound between them every few seconds.

     

    “I didn’t know you were so close with your Mom,” Carol says, her voice hesitant, knowing it’s her own fault, she tries to make it sound like an apology. 

     

    “You never asked,” Zosia bites back, clearly not hearing it. Carol looks away and Zosia licks her lips in regret. Carol watches her put a cigarette between her lips and light it. “My dad died when I was a kid, she never remarried or had any other children,” Zosia says quietly. “She wants me to move back to Poland.” 

     

    “Do you want to go back to Poland?” Carol asks, trying to keep the fear out of her voice at the words. Carol just settled in Chicago but she guesses she could move to Poland just as easily.

     

    Zosia huffs, smoke coming out of her nose, “No.” She shakes her head. “My life is here.”

     

    “Maybe your mom will move here then,” Carol says nervously, she can’t believe Zosia is still talking to her.

     

    “Maybe,” Zosia says, staring back out at the water. 

     

    “Can I ask you something?” Zosia takes a deep breath at Carol's words. 

     

    “You can ask, I may not answer.”

     

    “Fair enough.” Carol laughs, “It’s about something you said the other day,” Zosia looks over to her waiting for the question, she holds out her cigarette to Carol.

     

    “You look nervous,” Zosia explains. 

     

    Carol takes it from her and turns her head away as her lips wrap around the filter and her eyes close in bliss, if she really tries she can almost taste Zosia’s mouth against hers. She turns back to Zosia as she inhales, her eyes look a shade darker, Carol is sure hers are as well. “You said you’ve never had a girlfriend.” Zosia just nods and takes the cigarette back from Carol, very careful not to let their skin touch. They haven’t touched since the office. “So does that mean…” She knows it's ridiculous and probably not true but Carol hasn’t been able to stop wondering. “Was I… Was I your first?”

     

    Zosia chokes on the smoke she was inhaling and starts coughing up a fit as it exits back out of her mouth and nose. Carol starts to move closer but assumes Zosia wouldn’t like that so she stops a few inches away, just as Zosia’s coughing turns into laughter. She throws her head back and laughs with her whole body. Carol feels her cheek flush bright red and she tries to roll her eyes like she doesn't care which only makes Zosia laugh harder.

     

    Carol grabs the cigarette out of Zosia’s hand and takes a drag, shaking her head as she looks at the lake and Zosia’s laughter finally starts quieting. She leans over to Carol, her head hovering just over Carol’s shoulder. She reaches out and plucks the filter out of Carol’s hand, bringing their mouths just inches from one another as she moves. “No, Carol. You were not my first.” Zosia leans away with a smile but she stays sitting closer to Carol, just a foot of space between them now. 

     

    “Right,” Carol says. “I thought not.” She clears her throat, and looks over at Zosia who still looks amused. “Who was then?” Carol asks with an easy smile. 

     

    “Lena Nowicki,” Zosia says with a smug smile. “She was the prettiest girl at my college and I had just realized I was hot.”

     

    Carol interrupts her, “You ‘realized’ you were hot?” 

     

    “Mmhmm,” Zosia hums. “It was a very nice realization.”

     

    “I’m sure.” Carol laughs, she wants to hear every story that Zosia has to tell and she wants to feel her heart pound in her chest as she listens and her face ache from smiling too long for the rest of her life. “And you never made it official?” 

     

    “Of course not,” Zosia takes a drag. “She had a boyfriend of course.”

     

    “Of course,” Carol commiserates. 

     

    They spend the afternoon sitting there, the conversation is awkward occasionally, they steer around topics they both want to avoid and sometimes Carol can feel that all too familiar anger and tension hovering just at the edges of Zosia’s words but it never comes in. They spend the afternoon together and they don’t yell or scream or cry and it feels like hope.






    2029, 4 years, 11 months, 3 weeks, and 2 days since the Unjoining.

     

    Miles cancels again, this time for a real reason before anyone has arrived by the water. Zosia thumbs up his message and Carol waits by the door for her to send a text cancelling. Instead Zosia says she will be there in 30 minutes. Carol presses her forehead to her front door and stares down at the message trying to convince herself she hasn’t imagined it. 

     

    They have lunch together and Zosia tells her about her classes from that morning.





    2029, 4 years, 12 months, 2 weeks, and 1 day since the Unjoining.

     

    Movie tonight? Sent 3:28 pm

     

    Carol jumps as her phone vibrates on the desk beside her. She had been writing all day, for the first time in a while she had gotten lost in the words and she couldn’t even remember the last time she moved. 

     

    She grabs the phone and sees the message from Zosia. It’s to both Carol and Miles. The three of them went to a movie two weeks ago at the Music Box, apparently it was Zosia’s favorite theater in the city and Carol had never heard of it. Zosia had been so offended she had made plans for them to go before it seemed to register that she was actively making plans with Carol.

     

    By the time Zosia had realized it was too late and the three of them had a calendar invite confirmed. The theater was even better than Carol expected. It was an old building with only two theaters, the main one a large room with decor that had long gone out of fashion, there were balconies around the room over the seating, and red curtains covering the screen. On the left side of the room was an organ player, his hands moving across the keys and people applauded after each of his songs. Carol’s eyes had roamed around the room in wonder as Zosia and Miles pulled her towards their usual seats on the left side of the theater about 15 rows from the front. 

     

    Carol couldn’t even remember the last time she had gone to a movie theater, she had forgotten how much Zosia loved going to the movies in Berlin. She was always trying to drag Carol along with her or saying she was going to see something or other while Carol went to the residency, how could Carol have forgotten that?

     

    They had gotten a drink after the movie at the bar attached, Carol and Zosia a Coke and Miles a beer. Zosia spent 30 minutes talking about the films director and the cast and the other movies they had done that she loved. Miles had zoned out, clearly having heard similar rants one too many times but Carol had been entranced. She loved watching Zosia’s cheeks flush in excitement as she spoke and because she wasn’t breathing enough. When her hands would move wildly and eagerly as she tried to explain something but couldn’t find the words. When her hair would fall into her eyes because she was nodding her head eagerly at any response she got. 

     

    Carol had had to sit on her hands to keep herself from reaching out and tucking a strand behind Zosia’s ear. 

     

    Carol jumps at the opportunity to see Zosia like that again. She texts back after only a few minutes.

     

    I’m in. Sent 3:33pm

     

    Sorry darlings, can’t do it tonight! Super behind on grading :/ Sent 3:35 pm

     

    Carol’s stomach sinks, all her excitement fading, she gets out of her chair and throws herself down on the couch behind her, waiting for Zosia’s message. Maybe next week they’ll be able to see something, she only has to wait three more days for trivia night. 

     

    Whatever. The movie starts at 6:15, meet out front at 6. Sent 3:37pm

     

    Carol had dropped the phone onto her chest and honest to god squealed. 

     

    Things had been different between them since that lunch at the beach. When Miles went to the bathroom at trivia the table was no longer silent, when Carol asked Zosia a question, something simple, like how her day was, the younger woman would no longer glare at her but would answer! Sometimes Zosia would even ask Carol how her day was. Things weren’t perfect, there was still so much they hadn’t talked about, that Carol knew they would soon have to talk about, but not today.






    2030, 5 years, 4 months, 3 weeks, and 5 days since the Unjoining.

     

    Carol walked toward Dorothy’s and for the first time in months was full of anxious nerves. It had been four months since she first walked up to this bar and since then she had settled into a rhythm with both Miles and Zosia. Tonight, that rhythm was being interrupted. Miles has invited two of his and Zosia’s friends to join them for trivia. Carol has never met any of Miles or Zosia’s other friends; she has heard about the other people they hang out with but she has never been introduced to any. 

     

    It feels like a big step that she finally is, like the door to their world that has cracked open for Carol is now being pushed open wide to welcome her inside. Carol hasn’t met a new person in years now, the last new person she had met was Manousos and that wasn’t the smoothest start. Miles and Zosia know Carol, they know what she is like, and for some reason Carol has never really understood and might not ever understand, they still like her. They aren’t offended by her burst of anger or defensiveness, they’ve always been able to just brush right by it, unphased. But these new people… Carol needs to make a good impression, she needs them to like her. 

     

    Both of these friends were professors too, at different universities around the city. Carol has heard of them pretty regularly over the last few months. Zoë is an English professor just like Miles but unlike Miles she is more focused on classic literature rather than contemporary. From what Carol has heard, Zoë is a terrible gossip and that's half the reason Miles first became friends with her, to hear all about the English departments across the city and what their drama was. But that love of gossip had blossomed into a friendship and Zoë had introduced Miles and Zosia to her friend Tomas. Tomas taught in the humanities department of his university and was or so Miles said, gorgeous and quiet. 

     

    Carol dreaded meeting them. She had only just gotten comfortable around Miles and Zosia, she wasn’t sure she was ready to introduce more people into the mix. But Zosia had texted asking if it was okay they were invited and Carol couldn’t deny her anything. 

     

    So, Carol walks up to the bar with nerves rolling in her gut and walks around to the right side of the building expecting to find Miles and Zosia having their pre-trivia cigarette. Instead, she finds only Zosia. She looks up as Carol approaches, that sixth sense they both seem to have for one another never seems to have faded. 

     

    “Where’s Miles?” Carol asks, coming to a stop beside Zosia, leaning back against the building next to her. Zosia holds the cigarette between her lips and pulls out her pack, holding it out to Carol who takes one and reaches into the pocket of Zosia’s jacket. Carol’s fingers curl around the metal of Zosia’s lighter and pull it out. Zosia tucks the pack back into her pocket with a gentle dusting of pink across her cheeks. 

     

    Carol has been teetering over the line of flirting with Zosia lately, she just can’t help herself and Zosia isn’t telling her to stop.

     

    “You are going to bankrupt me if you keep stealing my cigarettes,” Zosia says, her voice teasing. Carol just hums, enjoying being alone with Zosia before their bubble bursts. “He’s inside with Zoë and Tomas, it’s busy tonight and he wanted to make sure we got a good table.” 

     

    Carol turns away and lets out a shaky exhale at the reminder of Zoë and Tomas, she wraps her free arm around her stomach trying to put pressure on it to keep it calm. Carol can feel Zosia’s eyes on her, tracking the movement. 

     

    “Are you nervous?” Zosia asks, turning toward Carol, her shoulder pressing into the wall.

     

    “No,” Carol scoffs haughtily, Zosia raises an eyebrow. “Yes,” Carol mumbles.

     

    “Don’t be. They’re harmless. Zoë will spend the whole night gossiping and asking you about your books,” Carol swallows heavily, her books are a topic they have firmly avoided for the last few months. “And Tomas will spend the whole night trying to remind Miles that he has a boyfriend while still flirting back.” Carol huffs out a laugh.

     

    “Sounds messy.”

     

    “Oh, extremely,” Zosia says sagely. “But that’s half the fun,” she smiles down at Carol. 

     

    “And do they—Do they know about us?” Carol gestures between the two of them with the hand holding the cigarette Zosia gave her. Her voice slightly breathless, she looks away after she speaks, her foot scrapping against the sidewalk.

     

    “What about us?” Zosia asks, she sounds closer than Carol remembers her being. She glances up and Zosia is just inches away, making Carol tilt her head back to see her clearly. 

     

    “That we’re…” Carol trails off lost in the intensity of Zosia’s eyes staring down at her. Zosia hasn’t mentioned what happened in the office or Carol’s confession or the fact that they sort of kissed and neither has Carol. Zosia doesn’t really initiate flirtation with Carol but she doesn’t stop Carol when she moves through her often clumsy attempts. But Zosia takes every opportunity to stare at Carol, it’s not exactly the way she used to, it's like her eyes are searching Carol for something. 

     

    Carol is sure she’s searching for anything to make her doubt Carol, to prove that Zosia is right and that Carol will run at the first sign of trouble. Carol had had to fight not to after their conversation in Zosia’s office, the urge to just leave had been so strong and Carol has felt it every now and then since, but the idea of being apart from Zosia again, and the knowledge that she would never be let back in keeps her in Chicago. She won’t ruin this last chance, she won’t prove Zosia right. Whatever Zosia is searching for when she looks at Carol, Carol will not let her find it. And if Zosia sees her fear she will also see that Carol is facing it and she is feeling it and still she is there, waiting.

     

    “That we’re… something,” Carol finishes lamely. Zosia chuckles, amused at Carol’s grand finish. 

     

    “No,” Zosia said. “They don’t know that we’re ‘something.’ They think we are old friends and we’ve just reconnected after a long time. We met them after everything,” Carol knows Zosia means the joining, another topic they haven’t touched in weeks. Zosia throws her waning cigarette onto the ground and stomps it out, her toes twisting over it. She puts her hands into the pockets of her jeans and steps away from the wall. She looks almost guilty or maybe embarrassed when she turns back to Carol, who watches curiously.

     

    “They will probably assume we have had sex,” Zosia says and Carol raises an eyebrow in shock, that is definitely a topic they have avoided. 

     

    “And why would they think that?” Carol asks, watching as Zosia inches further and further away towards the door, a darker blush forming on her cheeks.

     

    Zosia’s hand wraps around the door handle and Carol is sure she is going to walk in without answering but Zosia turns back to her just before she pulls the door open, all embarrassment or guilt gone from her face. Her eyes run from Carol’s feet up her body slowly. Carol feels the gaze on her skin, beneath her clothing like a physical touch. The nerves in her stomach turn into something warmer and buzzier. Zosia’s eyes burn into hers. “I have a very specific type,” Zosia opens the door and disappears inside, leaving Carol breathless against the building.





    After Carol had collected herself and taken a few steadying breaths she had gone into the bar and found the group at their usual table. Miles had introduced her enthusiastically, while Zosia watched over the rim of a glass she was sipping, and Carol tried to not ignore the newcomers in favor of just staring at Zosia. 

     

    The night went just how Zosia said it would, Zoë asked Carol a million questions about her publisher and for any gossip about authors which of course Carol had plenty to say about. She tells Zoë all about the times her work had been stolen by other authors and Zoë gasps at all the right moments and Miles gets animated about his own works being plagiarized. Zoë says she’ll start her first class of the week with a take down of these other books and Carol offers to buy her a drink with a laugh.

     

    Tomas is quiet, Carol doesn’t talk much directly to him but he smiles and laughs along with their conversation and when Carol gets a little too dark she can see a gleam in his eye that he likes the edge. She feels herself settle into the seat. Miles sends her a wink halfway through the night that she wants to hate him for but she lets the pride settle in her chest and her eyes seek out Zosia who is already watching her. She’s been watching her all night. First, with those same searching eyes and now with something Carol hasn’t seen from her in 25 years, desire.

     

    It makes Carol squirm in her chair and lose track of the conversion; she falls out of it as she stares across the table at Zosia, the two of them completely unaware of the people around them as they stare at each other. Carol can see Miles out of the corner of her eye glancing between them nervously. Carol watches Zosia’s chair jerk below the table and Zosia’s eyes break from hers to dart over to Miles in shock. They have one of their silent conversations and Zosia rolls her eyes when she looks away.

     

    Zosia rises from her chair, pushing her hair back over her head only for it to fall right back down into place against her cheeks and over her ears. “I’ll get the next round,” she says, a little breathless, her eyes meeting Carol’s for just a second before she turns and walks away. Carol watches her wondering if she should have followed her or not. If they were still in Berlin Zosia would have already pulled her into the alleyway or back to her apartment by now. 

     

    “So Carol,” Zoë says, her voice mischievous. Carol turns to her, she had almost forgotten Zoë and Tomas were even there. “You’ve known these two for a while right?” Zoë nods to Miles and Zosia’s empty seat.

     

    “Uh, yeah.” Carol says, looking over to Miles nervously. Carol picks up her seltzer, “Why?”

     

    “You must know something about Zosia’s mysterious lost love?” She leans in closer to Carol, stage whispering, “Miles over there is keeping his cards close to his chest.”

     

    Carol looks over to Miles for help but finds him very closely examining the trivia card that Carol has never seen him pick up in all the time they have been coming here. She scoffs at him, traitor, and turns back to Zoë. “Lost love?” Carol questions as thought she has never heard the words before.

     

    “Oh come on,” Zoë groans looking over to Tomas and hitting his shoulder in disbelief. “Look at her,” Zoë says, nodding her head toward the bar. Zosia leans against the wood top with a foot up on a metal bar set up as a foot rest for those sitting on their stools. She’s looking around the bar aimlessly waiting for the bartender. 

     

    Her hair has grown out a bit since they first reunited, it’s almost an inch longer, every day it gets closer to the length Zosia had when they first met and every day Carol counts down to the seconds until that length returns. It falls in soft, unprescribed waves around Zosia’s face. One of Zosia’s hands rests on the bar and somehow from this distance Carol can feel the tap of her rings against the wood as she moves. Zosia is wearing a black short sleeve button down that’s done up all the way and is just a bit oversized, and a pair of matching loose, black jeans that are rolled up an inch around her boots. She looks regal, like every light in the room is drawn to her and the air in the room is guiding you to look at her. 

     

    Carol turns away unable to keep looking at Zosia, it’s like staring at the sun. She turns back to Zoë who is watching her closely, Carol swallows her nerves. “Now look at them,” Zoë says, gesturing around the room. Carol had barely looked at the crowd of the bar, she barely looked any time they came here, always too absorbed in Miles and Zosia and trying not to say the wrong thing to Miles or Zosia. The rest of the room had never mattered much to Carol.

     

    But at Zoë’s prompt she looks around. Dorothy’s is a gay bar, more lesbian leaning, it's a cosy space, almost decorated like it’s someone’s basement with couches and low lights scattered around the room. There’s wood paneling and kitschy art on the walls and plants strewn around the space. Tall tables, short tables, and stools lining the bar. The room is filled with queer women. Maybe another reason Carol hadn’t really looked around the bar was the fact that there were so many queer women here. She hadn’t really spent much time around other lesbians besides Helen and they sort of, scared her? 

     

    Carol worried she didn’t know the inside jokes, that she didn’t care what her zodiac was and that she was old. She was so old compared to the people she saw coming in and out of the door every night, not even just age wise but just her life. She didn’t see the heaviness she felt around her shoulders in the faces of the people around her. She didn’t understand their freedom and their lack of concern. She couldn’t connect with it or maybe she just didn’t want to. 

     

    Looking around the room now Carol sees what Zoë sees. All around the room people are watching Zosia. 

     

    Not subtly either. A few tables away from Carol two women are huddled close together, their eyes running up and down Zosia like she’s a piece of meat as they giggle and whisper to one another. At the couches in the corner of the bar Carol can see a woman not so delicately stretching her arms up in a way that pulls her already short shirt up even further, exposing her, admittedly impressive, abs to Zosia. Carol’s eyes cross back over to the younger woman, still standing at the bar completely oblivious to all the staring and peacocking happening around her. 

     

    Carol watches a woman braver than the rest approach Zosia and stand beside her at the bar. She holds out a hand to Zosia, which she takes politely and the brave woman uses it to step in closer, whispering something in Zosia’s ear that makes her laugh.

     

    “Here we go,” Tomas says, taking a sip from his beer, all of them at the table now watching Zosia at the bar. Zoë groans next to Carol.

     

    “This happens all the time,” she explains turning to Carol, patting a hand on Carol’s forearm that rests on the table top.

     

    “What does?” Carol asks, she can feel Miles watching her. 

     

    “They always think they’ll be different,” Tomas says playfully. 

     

    “Half the city has been left broken hearted by our dear Zosia,” Zoë says.

     

    “And the other half is eagerly waiting to be left broken hearted,” Tomas laughs.

     

    Carol looks back out across the room and beyond the open salivating she sees eyes staring at Zosia with yearning, heartbroken girls unable to turn away as their friends try to pull them back into their conversations. Carol watches as one girl storms out of the bar with her friends trailing after her, they shoot glares over to Zosia who either pretends not to or just doesn’t notice. The woman Zosia had been speaking to is returning to her friends with a disappointed shrug of her shoulders. 

     

    Carol turns back to Zoë and catches her staring at the woman returning to her friend with something like commiseration. She raises an eyebrow, “And what half are you in?” Carol asks. Miles lets out a laugh he tries to hide by smacking a hand over his mouth extremely obviously.

     

    Zoë smiles dangerously, “The happily partnered half.” Carol breathes a little easier, “It would never work between us anyway.” Zoë takes a sip of her drink, “I’m not blonde enough.” She twirls a strand of her dark brown hair around her finger, and looks at Carol curiously. 

     

    “I guess not,” Carol says with a tense, but smug smile.

     

    “Carol,” their staring contest is broken by Zosia’s return, she holds out a glass to Carol, who takes it, letting her fingers brush against Zosia’s. 

     

    “Thank you,” Carol says, staring up at her from her seat. Zosia stares down at her with wide eyes, Carol leans back in her seat and lets her desire for Zosia shine through in her eyes. She watches as Zosia sees it staring back at her and tenses. Carol crosses one of her legs over the other and clenches them together at the feeling of Zosia watching her. Zosia’s eyes dart over to her legs, watching Carol tighten them under the table. When she looks back up at Carol her eyes are dark and her lips are slightly parted. 

     

    “Zamknij usta,” Close your mouth, Miles says, hiding the words around a cough. 

     

    Zosia startles and returns to her seat, Carol takes a sip of her drink and turns to Miles. “How was your class today, is that jock still giving you a hard time?” She asks innocently. Miles shakes his head at her, his eyes shining with glee and pride. 

     

    “He is an absolute menace…”

     

    The rest of the night is uneventful, they sink into the four teachers telling story after story of problem students and the crazy things they say and then tension that once filled their table is gone by the end of the night. When they leave Zosia still rides back with Carol, the car is still quiet but it isn’t uncomfortable. When they get out at Carol's apartment they stall on the sidewalk, their normal routine of Zosia pressing her head into Carol’s almost feels like too much tonight with the way they had been looking at one another. 

     

    Carol watches as Zosia puts a cigarette between her lips and lights it, the familiar tension between them rising on the sidewalk. Carol thinks that if she asked Zosia to come upstairs she might say yes. She could feel Zosia’s lips against hers again and her skin against her hands. They could sink into each other like they used to and disappear from the world for a few hours. 

     

    But there is too much they haven’t talked about. The longer they dance around one another the more Carol can feel their past lurking in the shadows. Zosia has started brushing it away anytime it comes up, she pushes past it changing the conversation before she can get angry or Carol can apologize again. At first, Carol had been grateful for the deflection, but now it feels like they have gone too long without speaking about it.

     

    It feels like Zosia is just doing what she always used to do for Carol, protecting her from herself. Letting her escape from the hard and scary thing by shoving down her own needs. Carol can feel how angry Zosia still is with her, she can feel how hurt Zosia still is. Carol can also feel how much Zosia loves her, how she aches for her. She doesn't know how she had been so oblivious to it in Berlin. It’s in every action, every glance, every moment. She would go upstairs with Carol and pretend that she wasn’t still angry for as long as she could keep it shoved down until she exploded or Carol exploded and they would be right back where they started. 

     

    So, Carol steps into Zosia’s space, she wraps a hand around Zosia’s forearm and rises onto the tips of her toes. She presses a lingering kiss to Zosia’s cheek, breathing in the smell of her skin as she does. Carol can feel Zosia turn her head towards her doing the same. Carol takes a step back, dropping Zosia’s arm from her hand and walking toward her front gate. “Goodnight,” Carol says, her voice tender. 

     

    “Goodnight,” Zosia calls back as Carol climbs the stairs of her building. Zosia doesn’t start walking away until Carol closes the door behind her. 






    2030, 5 years, 5 months, 2 weeks, and 26 days since the UnJoining.

     

    Things have been tense since the night at Dorothy’s. Carol still goes to trivia, she still sees movies with Zosia, she still gets lunch with the two of them at least once a week. But she feels unsettled. She feels restless. She’s been trying to get Zosia to talk about… Carol isn’t even really sure what, anything really. Carol wants to talk about the joining, she wants to talk about Berlin, she wants to talk about how crazy it is that Zosia has been in love with her for 25 years and how a few months ago Carol told her she loved her and now they just get lunch sometimes and stare at each other. 

     

    Carol has been picking fights, she knows she has but she can’t stop herself and Zosia refuses to bite. It’s unnerving. Carol doesn’t understand where all her anger has gone, where all her fight has gone. Carol doesn’t want to just forget all these things anymore, she wants to know the hard things, she wants to hear the hard things.

     

    Carol had been asked a few months ago by her publisher who had been asked by the Chicago Humanities Festival if she wanted to do a reading of her newest book. The book was still on the best sellers list and it had gotten very good press as one of the best books published after and about the joining. Carol was sent requests like this often and rejected most of them but this one felt different, it was bigger than anything she had ever done before and it would solidify her as a serious writer rather than just another romance novelist. 

     

    She had agreed eventually and tried not to think about all it would entail until the date started approaching. Carol would be reading a section of her book and then have a Q and A with another author they would pair her with. The main difference in this reading and all Carol’s others, was that usually her max crowd was about 50 people, this would be in a theater of 1,000. She would be on a big stage with microphones and spotlights and wouldn’t be able to see ten feet in front of her because of them. 

     

    Carol wasn’t going to tell Miles and Zosia, Zosia especially, about the reading. She figured they knew about it, there were signs all over the city about the festival with Carol's name on them, but no one ever brought it up. Just another thing on Carol's list of things that made her angry no one talked about. 

     

    After the last few weeks of stewing in her growing anger, Carol decided to invite them. She sent them both tickets and brought it up repeatedly anytime she saw either of them until they agreed to come. Carol thinks Zosia agreed mostly just to get Carol to stop talking. Carol wasn’t really sure what she wanted out of Zosia coming to this event, she just knew she couldn’t continue with how things had been. She wanted to talk, she wanted to fight, she wanted Zosia to let her in.

     

    When Carol had told all of this to Manousos he had not approved of her methods but he agreed that she and Zosia needed to speak. He asked Carol why she didn’t just try to directly ask Zosia to talk to her but Carol had already started tuning him out, pleased that he agreed with her and not Zosia. 

     

    Carol had sent Manny a ticket as well but he had said he was too busy to travel, Carol wasn’t really sure what he was busy with, he had gone back to managing the storage facility upon his return home, but she knew how important his daily routine was to him, how it had calmed him down after the unJoining and made him feel more settled after the upheaval, so Carol didn’t push him, maybe she would someday, but not today.

     

    Now, Carol waits backstage for her cue from the stage manger beside her. She can see just beyond the curtain to the edges of the crowd, she had been told the event was sold out and she couldn’t really believe it. Manny had texted her that morning saying good luck and she texted back an emoji of the finger, followed by the heart emoji. Miles had texted her just a few minutes ago saying that they had arrived. Carol hadn’t heard from Zosia directly in two days. Her nerves jumped at the knowledge that Zosia was in the room. Maybe this was a horrible idea?

     

    The stage manager tapped Carol on the arm and nodded to the stage, fuck. 





    The reading was easy enough, Carol had done so many over the years she fell into it easily enough, the crowd was much larger and the room was much bigger but Carol knew how to read, she knew when to pause for a reaction and she knew when to push through one. She lost herself in the words on the page and any fear or awareness of Miles and Zosia somewhere in the crowd was forgotten. 

     

    When Carol finished reading the room broke out in applause and a few scattered cheers that Carol smiled at humbly, internally she was soaking up the praise. The person beside her was Arnie Howard, another writer, Carol had read a few of their books after they were announced as her interviewer to make sure she was familiar with their work. It was much darker than Carol’s writing but she liked that about it. 

     

    “Thank you so much for that reading, Carol,” Arnie says as the crowd begins to quiet. “I’m so glad you chose that section. It's one of my favorite moments in the book.” They smile as the crowd calls out in agreement.

     

    “Really?” Carol asks, crossing one leg over the other, grabbing her glass of water and taking a sip. “Why’s that?” 

     

    “I mean,” they draw out the word. “There's a reason you're a best selling romance novelist.” Someone in the crowd whistles loudly and the whole room breaks out in a laugh. Carol just smiles knowingly out toward the audience, unable to see anyone but the front row. “I did want to start there actually if you don’t mind?” Arnie asks.

     

    “Of course,” Carol says, turning back to them.

     

    “This book is very different from your previous works. Wycaro is a little bit lighter as a series, the world is more fantastical and it always feels that in the end Lucasia and Raban will solve whatever mystery they are working against. In "Hi, Alice,” things are a little bit darker, the work plays much more with consent and individuality, it questions that notion of love that is so present in the Wycaro series, can you talk to us a little bit about what was compelling to you in that shift?”

     

    “Of course,” Carol leans forward, collecting herself for a moment. “I think after the ‘Joining,’” she puts finger quotes around the word and makes a face that the crowd laughs at. “We all had questions about these things, about what it meant to have experienced that, or in my case not experience that. I was also given the opportunity to stop hiding,” Arnie nods supportively. “And I chose to take it and write a book that felt a little more honest to my experience of romance. Raban and Lucasia are an ideal that I have never been able to reach and I don’t know if I ever will.” Her voice turns sad for a moment before she collects herself. “In Hi, Alice, I wanted to explore something more familiar, the kind of love that makes you hide and lie to yourself because you don’t want to lose it. The kind of love that you lose yourself in because it feels so good.”

     

    “The central relationship in the book is obviously between Alice and Yara and throughout the story we as the reader switch between, at least I did,” They laugh. “Supporting and being appalled by the relationship, on behalf of both of the characters!” Arnie exclaims. “They both take so much action to hurt the other but you still can’t help but hope for them to be together. What is it you think they find so irresistible about each other?”

     

    Carol laughs at the question, “I wonder that myself.” There's a chuckle around the room. “I think it’s because they never really get to know one another,” Carol says after a moment of consideration. “Yara is always at this distance from Alice, inaccessible in a way that makes it so difficult to know who she really is.” Carol licks her lips, “I think Alice likes that, she can project something onto it, she can create a world around this fantasy of a person who could be anything she wanted. She could be free in this fantasy in a way she couldn’t in the real world.” Carol pauses, her thoughts racing as she speaks, “There’s also a safety in knowing how much Yara loves her.” Arnie nods emphatically. 

     

    “Absolutely,” they say. 

     

    “Alice gets away with so much in the book, her anger, her aggression, her possessiveness, the fire, the car accident, all of it because she knows that no matter what happens; Yara won’t leave her. That she can’t leave her.” Out of the corner of her eye Carol sees someone stand up out of the seat and start climbing the stairs to the exit. She knows it’s Zosia immediately, Carol can see Miles trailing after her, gathering their coats in his arms. Zosia shoves the door open, the metal creaking as she does, making more of the audience turn to look. 

     

    Carol watches as Zosia turns back to look at her once more before leaving. She wears the same expression Carol saw on her face as she walked out of her bedroom in Berlin with Zosia still lying in her bed. Carol can hear that Arnie is asking her another question but it doesn't register, she stares after Zosia who lets go of the door close behind her and after a few moments the room plunges back into darkness. 

     

    Carol keeps staring at the door after it closes, in her mind all the light in the room cuts out and everyone inside except for her disappears. She sits there alone on the stage and keeps speaking. “She uses Yara’s love against her because she knows Yara will keep letting her, because people have done the same to Alice her whole life, and because it’s the safer way to live. She hides from how devastatingly in love she is and she makes it all Yara’s problem. Alice watches herself as she keeps taking and taking from Yara without giving anything in return and she thinks about her mother and she hates herself.” Carol takes a breath, “The end of the book is a message of hope, a break in the cycle, Alice refuses to continue and she leaves Yara to find a way to restore her to herself, not to save the world but to save the girl.”

     

    “Carol?” 

     

    She’s pulled out of the darkness and the fantasy by Arnie. “Carol?” They say again. 

     

    She turns back to the door, “Sorry, I just got lost in…” she trails off, her mind still not fully present. 

     

    “Don’t worry about it,” Arnie says, “How about we move on to my next question?”

     

    “Sure,” Carol says, her eyes still on the door.





    The rest of the reading passes in a haze, Carol walks home in a haze, she goes to sleep thinking about all the things she should have done differently, she thinks about calling Manny but doesn't want to hear an ‘I told you so’ she thinks about calling Miles but knows he’s probably with Zosia. 

     

    Carol sleeps for only two hours that night. She wakes up to no texts from Miles or Zosia but one from Manny asking how the reading went. She calls him and tells him about the night, he doesn’t say I told you so but Carol can hear it ringing in her head.

     

    She doesn’t hear from Miles or Zosia for two weeks.

     

     

    Chapter End Notes

    Carol and Zosia and their emotional support gays make me so happy

    Chapter 17: Part Two: The Joining

    Chapter Notes

    2030, 5 years, 5 months, 4 weeks, and 20 days since the Unjoining.

     

    The two weeks without Miles and Zosia are miserable, Carol wanders her apartment and for the first time regrets getting something so big and so empty. Miles texts her on Friday and tells her not to come to trivia, Carol asks him if Zosia is alright and he doesn't respond. Carol regrets inviting them, she regrets not thinking things through more. But she also feels almost glad that Zosia is angry at her again, it feels more honest. She can feel anticipation coiling in her stomach for the moment they reunite and the fight she expects them to have. 

     

    Carol feels excited by the idea of finally getting to talk about all the things they have been avoiding. She sits at her desk waiting by the phone. She cooks dinner while waiting by the phone. She watches TV waiting by the phone. She lies awake at night waiting by the phone. As time passes the excitement curdles into guilt just like it always does. She acted rashly, she acted without thinking. She made yet another decision about something without thinking about or consulting Zosia. She forced Zosia to face something maybe she wasn’t ready to. 

     

    Wasn’t she supposed to be waiting for Zosia to be ready? Was she supposed to be proving that she could be patient and reliable? As the days go by Carol becomes more and more scared that the next time she sees Zosia, she will take Carol that she never wants to see her again. Carol isn’t sure she could do it anymore, not after the last few months, not after getting to know Zosia even deeper, not after getting to be around her and feel what it’s like to be in her presence without being scared of what's waiting around every corner. 

     

    She should have waited, she should have swallowed her anger and her desires and waited. They had been seeing movies together, they had been having lunch together, how could Carol have started taking all of that for granted so quickly. How had she let herself be blinded by how lucky she was, how had she let herself be blind to how hard Zosia was working to let her in, to try to forgive her, to try to find a way to let Carol back in. 

     

    Instead, she had thrown it all in Zosia’s face in front of a room of people who thought it was just a story to be debated, who thought the character Carol had based on herself was some beautiful romantic lead and not the girl who had broken Zosia’s heart one too many times. She always found a way to ruin everything good in her life, she always found a way to hurt the people around her, she was always asking too much and not thinking enough. 

     

    Knock. Knock. Knock.

     

    Carol is startled out of her thoughts as the sound travels across her living room from the front door. She stares at the door for a moment, Carol knows who is on the other side, she can practically feel her. She wants to savor this moment before Zosia tells her she doesn't want to see her again. 

     

    Zosia knocks again.

She’s wearing a black shirt, dark jeans and her carhartt jacket, her hands tucked into the pockets. “Can I come in?” Zosia asks, her voice hoarse like she hadn’t spoken yet today.

    They spend the rest of the night on Carol's couch, always touching in one way or another, talking about the joining. It’s the first in a long line of conversations, it’s hard and it’s painful, they both lash out and they both get defensive but they stay and they talk.

    They talk about when Zosia first walked through the gate to Carol and Helen’s yard.

This one is looser than the last. A washed blue fabric, maybe denim? Carol can’t tell and she’s not about to touch it to find out. Zosia’s still wearing heels making her even taller than Carol. Don’t they remember her complaining about that constantly? Zosia’s pants are belted and her shirt is tucked in with more buttons undone than Carol is comfortable with. She stares at Carol with that fond expression. Like she just couldn’t wait for the moment Carol opened the door.

     

    “What are you doing here?” Carol asks, indignant. 

     

    “Hi, Carol.” Zosia says her voice is more familiar than usual. “Sorry it took so long.” She says, Carol’s eyebrows furrow, sorry what took so long? As soon as the question enters her mind it is answered. Zosia’s long arm reaches out towards her, her palm up and resting, perfectly cradled in it as a grenade. 

     

    Carol stares at it for a moment confused before remembering her words on the phone. Jesus Christ, she’s even worse at sarcasm than the real Zosia had been. Carol raises her eyebrows at it trying not to let her surprise show. She nods sagely, “A hand grenade.”

     

    “Yes,” Zosia smiles at her widely, her expression slowly fading as she speaks and as she takes in the expression on Carol's face. “We thought you were probably being sarcastic, but we didn’t want to take the chance.” The smile fighting to return, has a hand grenade ever been delivered with such excitement, such trepidation. 

     

    Zosia’s eyes widen in panic as Carol doesn't respond, she presses her eyebrows together in confusion. “Were you being sarcastic?” She asks like it's a matter of national security, her face both embarrassed and scared.

     

    Carol stares back unblinking, leaning her shoulder against the door frame. Zosia would hate this version of herself.

     

    Zosia looks away, a blush blooming on her cheeks. Carol has never seen Zosia blush before, her first thought is that it’s adorable. Her second thought is that she never should have opened the door. “Right. Of course you were,” Zosia says shyly. She laughs nervously, looking down at the grenade in her hand and back up at Carol, “Do you want us to take…” She pauses, stopping herself. “You know what? We’re just gonna… We’ll get rid of it.” She finishes with a pleased smile. She turns to leave, pausing to look back over at Carol. Her voice lowering as she speaks, “Feel better, Carol.” She says like she means it, like she cares.

     

    She starts walking away, and Carol thinks about the empty house behind her. Thinks about the night of TV and drinking ahead of her. Thinks about the waste of time, maybe she could invite Zosia in. Maybe she could try to find out what these people want, maybe she could find out how they work, maybe she could still stop them.

     

    “Hey.” She calls out after Zosia, who immediately stops walking and turns back like an over excited puppy. “Do you maniacs drink?” Carol asks cautiously. Zosia looks down at the grenade again. “It’s okay.” Carol nods, “You can bring the hand grenade.” She steps into the house, pressing her back against the door walking it back, holding it open in invitation to Zosia.

     

    In the low light of the night, she swears she sees Zosia’s eyes shining at her just like they used to. She swears she feels them against her skin the way she always could when they landed on her. But she blinks and it's gone. 

     

    Zosia walks back up the path toward her. Carol stays still, her back against the door as Zosia walks by her. “Thank you, Carol,” Zosia murmurs, her accent thickening around Carol's name. Carol flinches at the sound, her back jerking against the door, she prays that Zosia doesn't notice as she walks into the house.

     

    Carol stands there for a moment, she takes a deep breath and closes the door behind her. Zosia stands a few paces away, watching her. Carol pushes past her, not interested in the staring contest, her cheeks not feeling hot, if they were she would blame it on the vodka.

     

    She points to the den and tells Zosia to wait there. Zosia is already moving before Carol finishes, she walks through the house like she has been in it a thousand times. Carol grabs the open bottle off the coffee table and two glasses from the bar and tries not to think about how that could be true. 

     

    Carol sets the glasses on a table between two arm chairs in the den. She pours a finger’s worth of vodka into each and sets the bottle down nearby, she has a feeling she’s going to need it. The bottle goes down harder than she intends, Carol's distracted, she's trying to focus her mind as it runs wild at the fact that Zosia is in her house, that Zosia is sitting in her chair. 

     

    Zosia sets the grenade down on the table between them, on top of a stack of books. Carol takes a sip and settles into her own seat. She watches Zosia grab her glass as Carol pulls a leg up, bending it at the knee and pulling it close. She needs the barrier, the table doesn't feel like enough.

     

    Zosia leans back in her chair and stares at the glass. “Does the whole world get drunk when you drink?” Carol asks, committed to this ‘interrogation.’ “Like, does some six-year-old in Sri Lanka slur his words when you knock one back?”

     

    “No, it doesn't work like that.” Zosia’s voice still has the horrible—wonderful—new rasp to it. 

     

    “Hmm.” Carol says, taking a sip to steady herself. “How do you say cheers in Sanskrit?” She asks, needing the distraction.

     

    “Śubhamastu,” Zosia says, her face and eyes staring at Carol with so much fondness. “Roughly it means, ‘may everyone be blessed.’” Her hands outstretched to emphasize the words. A happy smile on her face. Carol watches, unnerved.

     

    “Well, then. Shoot… Shoo…” She stares at her glass in confusion, she gives up. “Shoopy shoop shoop.” She says, unashamed, as she raises her glass to the body of her ex-lover she abandoned in Berlin. She downs the contents. Zosia takes a delicate sip.

     

    Carol sets her glass down and pours herself more. 

     

    “You know the word ‘vodka’ is a diminutive of voda, meaning ‘water.’” Carol’s eyes watch in judgement as Zosia speaks. She was twisting the cap back onto the bottle but decides against it as she listens to Zosia ramble, better to have easy access. “Very similar to the Latin aqua vitae, literally ‘water of life.’” Carol leans back in her chair, grabbing her glass and scratching at her face to contain herself. “This becomes the Scandinavian akvavit.” Zosia’s face is so expressive, so alive, so entertained by what she is saying. “Although the drinks are very different.” She clarifies like any of this matters to Carol. Carol watches with a hand over her mouth. 

     

    She can see Zosia pacing in front of her couch after a soccer practice, ranting about positions and rosters and formations. Her accent makes the already unfamiliar words even harder to understand. She can see Zosia slipping into Polish when she can’t think of the word in English. She can see Zosia looking to her for agreement. She can feel the ghost of her smile as she watches Zosia’s face light up explaining what off-sides means to Carol who didn’t catch a word of it. 

     

    She watches Zosia rant about the origin of words, her face lighting up in the same way as it used to. Her voice takes on a childlike quality of wonder, delighting in the information. She watches the echo of who Zosia used to be as it ripples across the surface of her face and Carol swallows down the memories and tries not to think about how she is the only one left to remember her.

     

    “Fun fact, whiskey has the same root meaning. It’s Scots Gaelic from uisce beatha.”

     

    “You don’t say.” Carol teases to keep herself from breaking apart. Zosia nods, seemingly unaware of Carol's mood shift.

     

    “What gives this particular brand its distinctive smoothness, it’s distilled from both potato and corn.” She looks satisfied. “It is slightly alkaline. Do you taste that?” The brand is ruined for Carol forever, she takes a big sip and looks back up at Zosia as she points at Carol expectantly. “Lower-shelf alcohol tends to be acidic.” Zosia explains, as if Carol doesn't know her way around the shelves of a liquor store. “The chief distiller learned the process from his grandfather.” Zosia finishes with a fond smile, almost wistful.

     

    “And now you learned it by stealing it out of his brain.” Carol bites out at her, feeling vindictive, feeling angry she let Zosia into her house, or whoever this is into her house. Zosia stares back, she blinks at Carol and just keeps staring. 

     

    Carol clears her throat and looks away, unable to maintain eye contact. She sets her glass on the table and tries to get herself back on track. “How long do I have left before you turn me into a worker bee?” Carol asks, her voice fearful.

     

    Zosia shakes her head, uncomfortable with the question. “It’s a hard thing to predict.” She delays. “Scientific advances tend to ebb and flow.” She’s never felt more unfamiliar to Carol than in this moment. Her obfuscation in such obvious contrast to Zosia’s blunt honesty.

     

    Carol winces and shakes a finger at Zosia. “That’s not an answer. How long?” She questions again. 

     

    “We’re working round the clock.” An essential service. “It could be as soon as a couple weeks, or it could take months or longer.” Zosia says vaguely. 

     

    Carol nods, frustrated and distrustful, “That’s quite the range for someone who knows everything that there is to know.” Her hands gesture around them as she speaks. Her eyes narrow, suspicious of how much Zosia is withholding from her. “Regardless, sooner or later, I’m fucked,” Carol says simply. She leans in, grabbing her glass back up off the table and taking a gulp.

     

    “Sorry, Carol,” Zosia says with not a hint of empathy in her voice. “We have a biological imperative.” What does that even mean?

     

    “You people make no goddamn sense.” Carol says groaning around the words, she sets the glass down once again this time it sings around the force. “Do you know that?” She gears up, “‘We wanna make you happy,’ you say. ‘Your life is your own,’ you say.” Her voice, her eyes begging Zosia to understand. “And ‘agency.’ I've got all this agency, but…” She stutters, “I… I mean I guess I have agency just until I don't?" She probes, needing to understand, needing to prove how hypocritical they are being and having no one else to say it to than Zosia.

     

    Zosia watches her with unnerving calm. “Carol...” she says, Zosia leans in closer, her elbows resting on the arm of her chair. “If you were walking by a lake, and you saw somebody drowning, would you throw ‘em a life preserver?” Carol shakes her head in confusion, not understanding at all what the question has to do with anything. “Of course you would. You wouldn’t think, you wouldn’t wait, you wouldn’t try to get consensus on it. You’d just throw it.”

     

    Carol closes her eyes in shock, not sure what to say to that, “So now I’m drawing?” She forces out. Zosia smiles at her sadly. 

     

    “You just don’t know it,” Zosia says with a loving shake of her head. 

     

    Carol looks away, she can’t look at her anymore. “Well, you people are brainwashed, is what you are.” She stands up, huffing in disbelief. Zosia leans back in her chair and just watches. “I mean what could possibly be so great about this mind meld of yours?” She questions, her voice shaky under the weight of Zosia’s stare, her hands gesturing widely, trying to help herself get the words out. “Actually,” She laughs, closing her eyes. “Let me guess.” Carol steps forward leaning down slightly in faux excitement. “It’s… uhh,” her voice searching. “It’s all beautiful scenery, and you feel nothing but contentment.” Her voice taking on an airy quality as she speaks, Zosia chuckles and looks down at her glass before those eyes return to Carol. “Just wave after wave of bliss and peace and everything is perfect.” Carol’s voice grows quieter as she feels something she hasn’t felt in twenty years.

     

    They’ve finally done it, they’ve gotten Zosia’s eyes back. Whatever they were missing, whatever secret quality it was about them that had faded is back. Carol can feel the heat of Zosia’s eyes on her as she speaks, she can feel the warmth of them like it was yesterday. The memory of Zosia looking at her like this, every second they were together, no longer compares to the real thing. Carol can feel the way her body wakes up under the stare, the way it makes her knees feel weak. She stumbles over her words. “It's… its… its… like living inside a postcard,” Carol takes a breath, her voice uncertain. “Every second of every day.”

     

    Zosia watches her just like she used to when Carol would rant about a book she hated or about something stupid Miles said that pissed her off. She watches Carol with those huge brown eyes like she’d move heaven and Earth to make Carol happy, like she’d burn every copy of the book that offended Carol, like she’d cut Miles out of her life if it saved Carol from ever speaking to him again. Like all she wants to do is get out of her chair and grab Carol’s head between her hands and press their lips together. The force of it knocks the wind out of Carol, her body, her shoulders, her anger it all feels heavy under its gaze, it all feels meaningless.

     

    She forces herself to go on, fighting to keep her anger from fading. “Like you’re taking a hike in the woods, and there’s a warm rain, and the trees are so tall you can’t even see the tops. She looks up to the ceiling as she speaks, needing a break, needing to not be looking at Zosia. “Or you're having coffee on the canals in Amsterdam, and it’s like you’re in a coffee commercial.” She sees Zosia handing her a coffee while they sit by the river in Berlin. “Or you’re taking a walk at sunset on the most beautiful beach in Croatia." She sees Zosia, leaning back, smoking as they watch the sun set in Tempelhof, oblivious to Carol watching her. “Or you're in Berlin, standing in a bar with your friends…” She trails off, she doesn't know why she's still speaking, her voice sounds far away even to her own ears.

     

    “And all you can think about is leaving.” Zosia says, her accent heavier than Carol has heard it since she walked into Carol’s backyard. Her eyes snap to Zosia, jarred out of her musings. “And going home to me.” Carol stares at Zosia in horror, she forces her eyes to the ceiling trying to control herself. The humiliation of Zosia’s words filled her with rage.

     

    “Don’t ever,” Carol’s voice shakes with anger, “Talk about Berlin to me again.” She steps closer to Zosia, leaning down so they are at eye level. Her eyes hard as she stares at Zosia, whose face has dropped out of the loving smile, her eyes back to being empty, just like that. Zosia looks away from Carol as she leans even closer, the anger radiating off of her dangerously. 

     

    Carol reaches over and grabs the grenade off the table. Needing something to distract herself, needing something to help her forget the last five minutes. She holds it out in front of her, between herself and Zosia, who looks up again as she speaks. “You are a bunch of miind-fuckers.” Carol spits at her. She turns her back and over to the windows, needing distance. She fidgets with the grenade in her hands.

     

    She tries to pull the pin, it won't budge, Zosia watches her surreptitiously, suitably quiet after Carol's display. Carol looks over at her as she tries once more to pull out the pin. She scoffs out a laugh as she looks down at the weapon in her hands. “Wow, you got this thing really jammed in there, don’t you?” She taunts, as she finishes it finally slides out, her arms jerking at the unexpected release.

     

    Zosia stands up quickly, her drink clattering on the table beside her. Carol stares at the pin in confusion. “Please,” Zosia says cautiously. “Be careful with that.”

     

    Carol scoffs at her, “Right. Like you would give me a real hand grenade.” She gestures skeptically with the grenade in one hand and the pin in the other. The moment she finishes speaking, she sees something fly through her field of vision. Her stomach drops.

     

    She looks back at the bare grenade in her hand, and stares at it in fear. Zosia steps forward, “Carol,” she says apologetically. Zosia grabs the grenade out of Carol’s hand, “If we may.” She throws it hard through the window behind Carol who watches in shock at the scene. Carol throws her arms over her head to shield herself from the impending blast. 

     

    Zosia’s hands grab her hips with the confidence of a woman who has held them so many times before, she turns Carol around, grabs Carol's shoulder and pushes her to the ground with the weight of her body falling on top of Carol’s. Shielding her from the blast as glass and wood go flying around the room.

    They talk about the books.





    “You,” Carol barks out, pointing at the guy in bike shorts. “Shorty.” He nods, smiling. “The rest of you just take a… coffee break or whatever floats your boat.” She says uncertainly, “Come with me,” she says to bike shorts, waving him into the house, not waiting to see if he follows, knowing he will.

     

    “Happy to.” He calls out to her.

     

    Carol closes the door behind them and leads him into the dining room. “Have a seat… What’s your name?” She asks, gesturing to a chair. He stares back confused for a moment, “Your name?” Carol asks again, annoyed.

     

    He stares back blankly, “well…” his voice is nervous, “This individual,” his hands motioning up and down his body, Carol raises an eyebrow in displeasure. “Went by Lawrence J. Kless.” Carol nods, just going with it for now. “Or Larry to his friends, of which he had many.” He smiles like he’s happy for Larry, as if he isn’t Larry.

     

    “Just…” Carol points to the chair again, “Have a seat.” 

     

    They sit at the same time, across the table from one another. Carol clears her throat nervously, her arms on the table, she claps her hands together trying to be patient and not jump in too fast, not wanting to be rude. “Do you like my books?” She asks enunciating very intentionally, her fingers interlock on the surface of the table.

     

    “Oh, We love your books,” He says emphatically.

     

    She raises her eyebrows, “What do you love about them?” She pokes.

     

    “Everything. Your books are an expression of you. And we love you.” That fucking serene smile.

     

    Carol hums, not interested in it. “Need you to be more specific. Character arcs. Plot turns.” She prompts.

     

    “Oh, yes.” His voice eager once more, “We love the character arcs and the plot turns.” 

     

    She nods, it’s like pulling fucking teeth, “Which ones?” Carol smiles falsely, barely holding herself back from leaping across the table at him.

     

    “All of them.” He says again, with an oblivious shake of his head. She stares at him with barely contained anger.

     

    “No offense, Larry, but I think you’re bullshitting me.” Carol smiles, mockingly, knowing they won’t be able to help but appease her, “Sounds like you haven’t even actually read my work.”

Gosh,” he looks around, searching, “Where do we even begin?” He sucks in a breath, thinking. “In your first novel, we greatly admire the description of Lucasia’s gown on page two.” His eyes closed in bliss, “‘Gossamer threads of the finest shang spun silk shimmered like spun gold,” Carol watches him speak, unsettled at the perfect quote, “As if the sun itself were showering Lucasia’s shoulders with resplendent kisses.” She sits up straighter and he sighs out a laugh, “That made quite a few of us tingle.” He leans in conspiratorily.

     

    “Okay,” Carol says uncomfortably. She leans back in her seat, readying herself for the next assault. “Ha.” She says as she tries to think of what to ask him next. What would be most revealing? “How would you say my work compares to…” She stalls seeking,  “Shakespeare?” She asks hesitantly.

     

    “Equally. Equally wonderful.” He says without an ounce of facetiousness.

     

    “You love my books as much as…” She stares at him blankly, “Macbeth?” He nods, “Or Much ado?” He nods again.

     

    “Yes. And Henry VI, parts one, two and three.” 

     

    “Well, I mean, what about a crowd-pleaser?” She says sarcastically, “Romeo and Juliet?”

     

    “Wonderful,” He says, “Have you read the first quarto of 1597?” He genuinely asks her that. “No, you haven’t,” he answers himself as Carol purses her lips; she hates them. “But you must. We’ll bring you the original.” He says decidedly.

     

    She ignores that, and leans forward once more, forearms on the table. “So, Larry, are you telling me that you love the Wycaro series as much as the most famous English language play of all time?” She can’t believe that could be true.

     

    “Carol,” he says with a loving shake of his head, “When we think about a passage from one of your wonderful—”

     

    “Stop using that word,” She says, sick of the way he draws it out. 

     

    He smiles and leans forward, almost teasingly, “From one of your books. We experience it through many eyes. Many hearts.” He pauses smiling at her, undaunted by her frown. “Do you remember a woman named Moira McAllister?” He asks.

     

    “Uh, Crazy Moira from Kansas City that sent me these dumb crocheted hats?” She says harshly, with a twitch of her head. “Right, of course.” She says, “She’s one of you now.” 

     

    “Yes,” he says softly, Carol hums in acknowledgement. “Before she found your books, Moira was deeply depressed. In fact she was going to…” He trails off, the hard expression on Carol’s face fades to one of guilt. “You gave her something to live for.” Carol swallows hard at the words. “You made her life better.” Carol bites back a pleased smile, “That’s how we see your work, and you. Through Moira’s eyes.” She looks away, not wanting to be as affected by what he says as she is.

     

    “What did Helen think of my books?” Carol asks with an excited smile, knowing it will make him uncomfortable. He stares back silently, she raises her eyebrows, suddenly regretting the question, “Tell me.”

     

    “Helen loved you very much.” He says back in that same fucking patronizing voice. 

     

    “Not what I asked, Larry.” She says irritated, “Spit it out. What did Helen think of the Wycaro books?” 

     

    “Carol, you expressly told us that Helen is off-limits.” he says delayed, deflecting. 

     

    “Yeah,” Carol draws out the word. “But you know what? Special dispensation this one time. What did Helen think of my Wycaro books?” Carol doesn’t even remember why she brought him into the house anymore. “Larry?” 

     

    “She thought they were… Harmless.” His voice was hesitant, almost scared.

     

    “Harmless?” Carol questions, keeping her voice flat.

     

    “‘Cotton candy’ was the phrase she used in her mind. Mostly.” 

     

    “‘Cotton candy.’” Carol exclaims. She rolls her shoulders.

But really…” He trails off in a chuckle, before pausing as Carol stares at him biting her lip in anger, he leans forward finally returning to the topic at hand. “Helen thought you knew how to please your audience. And she was so grateful for the beautiful life the two of you shared, that Wycaro provided.” He says, emphasizing, “She was so proud of you.” She hates them for that.

     

    “What did Helen think of my unpublished novel?” She forces herself to ask, in for a penny, in for a pound.

     

    He sighs happily, “Bitter Chrysalis.” His eyes slipped closed in pleasure as he said the words. 

     

    “What did she think?” Every muscle in her body is tight.

     

    She’s met with silence.

     

    “Carol. This line of inquiry isn’t… Can we talk about something else?” He asks, clearly uncomfortable. The ‘We only want to make you happy’ heavy in the air between them.

     

    “I wanna know,” Carol demands. She tilts her head to the side, “If you tell me it’ll make me happy.” She feels gross.

     

    He looks down at the table and swallows before he speaks, “Helen thought it was fine.”

     

    “Fine as in good?” She says desperate for more, desperate for hope. “Or fine as in…” She trails off, shrugs, “‘Meh?’”

     

    “Yeah, Meh.” He says, cringing at his agreement.

     

    She can’t help but raise her voice slightly at that. Her hands raising off the table in shock, “What about the last 200 pages?” Surely, Helen liked those.

    “It’s 489 pages long,” She forces out between clenched teeth.

    “If she hated it so much…”

     

    “She didn’t hate it.” Larry interrupts.

     

    “If she hated it so much, why did she tell me to publish it?” She asks confused, feeling betrayed.

     

    “Because she talked to Val, and they agreed that it wouldn’t hurt your career.” He says smiling, like that's some huge consolation. “And it would make you happy.”

     

    “Why did she not like it?” Carol’s voice shakes as she asks. 

     

    He licks his lips and doesn’t say anything. Carol leans her head closer, tilts it to the right sharply. He swallows again. “She felt uncomfortable reading it.” He says cryptically.

     

    “‘Uncomfortable?’” Carols repeats. 

     

    He stares at her with something akin to pity, but not quite that. “She felt the love interest with ‘warm brown eyes’ and ‘wavy brown hair’ and ‘a thick unplacable accent’ bore a striking resemblance to your descriptions of Zosia and she felt,” He considers his word choice carefully,  “uncomfortable continuing.” He finishes, his eyes searching her face in concern.

     

    Carol feels the blood drain from her face, she purses her lips and looks down at the table. Helen never said a word about any of this to her. She never expressed any resentment or discomfort about Zosia to her, ever, over the course of twenty years. 

     

    Why wouldn’t she have just said something? Carol wishes more than anything that Larry was gone and it was just her and Helen at this table again. 

     

    But Helen is gone and Larry is here. Or whatever Larry is is here.

     

    She looks back up at him. The door is cracked open, she can’t help but want to peek inside.

     

    “Did Zosia read my books?” Her heart was pounding in her ears.

     

    “Of course, Zosia loves the books.” Carol winces, shaking her head, that’s not what she meant.

    He smiles apologetically, “Yes. Zosia read them before.” Carol's heart jumps into her throat. She isn’t sure if she’s excited or terrified. She shifts in her seat, needing something to do. Her hands grasp each other tighter on the table, the skin around them turning white.
